[—Bands of music are fothAden EQ. play on most :litthe large bridges of the wetld. A cOnstant succession ef sound waves, especially such as cgme from the playing of a band, will excite the wires.to vibration. At first 4the vibrations are very slight, but they will increase as the sound waves continue to come. The prtncipal reasan why bands are not allowed to plaY when crossing certain'bridges, the suspension bridge at Niagara, for instance, is that if followed by processions of any kind • they will keep step with the music, and this regular step would cause the wires to vibrate. At suspension bridges military companies are not allowed to march accross in regular step, but break ranks. The regular trotting gait of a laree dog across a suspension bridge is more daneer- ous to the bridge than a heavily loadel waggon drawn , • by a team of large horses. .‘ ELECTRIC SNOW. tattlA r r`,l ,05.1 -0 ,amarkahle snow4torm, atoid by,

Lieut. John P. Finley, one of the best informed meteo-- rolagists in the United States, who encounted the storm In making an ascent of Pike's Peak. ~Ile says the atormt• could be described as a ", shower of cold fire." In reality' it was so charged w;.th electricity as to present a scene - more easily imagined than described, At Arst the flakes' ,only discharged their tiny lights on, coming in con- tact with the hair of the mute on which the lieutenant was mounted. Presently they began coming thicker and ,faster,each flake emitting its spark as it sank intogrifts of the snow or settled on the clothing of the lieutenant or the hair of the mule. I made further investigations, and am convinced that if the. Sastmlis had been surrounded by an impassible wall their rude but glad: existence would have gone on indefinitely. Shut out the Kurds, and the Armen- ians would have no use for gunpowder. Their fields and their flocks supported a large and buoy population; the mountains were rough and rocky, but the valleys were fertile and sheltered. Peaceful among themselves, able to win a Sufficient sustenance by constant struggle with nature, the only thing they have had to fear has tieen the rapations Kurd and the cruel Turk. Though not luxurious, life in Sasun was physically comfortable. Open handed hespitality_and provision for the poor were

rnienit!.7.•l

SO charitable, infant, were the Sasun people known to be that the poverty-stricken villagers of Moosh plain were frequently in the habit of going there to beg, and they were never refused; but now the wretched surviv- ors of that once prosperous conernunity, are canuielled to seek their bread in tags at the dooti-dt 'these to whom not long since they were giving- eliiiiilftWeiiiselves. There was considerable variety in the occupations which followed one another in rapid succession through- out the year. No drones were tolerated in that busy hive, and in all their toils men and women stood shoul- der to shoulder. It would be difficult to decide which sex bore the harder burden even from the standpoint of physical strain. Take the care of the flocks for instance. To the men was intrusted the task of pasturing and protecting them, but it was the women who did all the milking and who made all the butter and cheese: The shearing of the sheep was done by the men, but he women a aahed,carded,and spun the wool into thread „which was then.woven into.excellent cloth by the men, as they sat at their heavy' and , cumbrous looms; and from this homespun the wornen • were expected to rake all the garments of the limisehbld. [Even their cottos clothipg went through the 4arne stages ,the crude gotten beim brought to them in bales from the Muth- ThitOUgh the long.winter montlis..the :immense flocks of sheep- were guarded by the men in the sheltered spots, often quite a distance from the VillanS, but the cattle and horses were kept ilw Centres, and the woinen were ex- pected to aid in wateringand caring for them. During the dry unoomfortable months Of summer practleally all the &impale and a large part of the population migraped to the cool tipper slopes of the mountains, where springs and melting anew keep the grass always green. Agricultural .operations were confined to raising 'wheat, millet, barley, .and rye, together with -a variety of vegetables, [ such as potatoes, tomatoes, squashes, clicunabers, turnips, and a large coarse variety of bean called-4! paklah." Around their homes they had such fruit trees as the apple, pear, cherry, apricot, and quince. In the lower and warmer valley of Dalvori the fig alio flourished and vineyards were very abundant. In the course of the massacre all the fruit trees and vine- yards throughout the region were systematically cut down. Magnificent walnut trees were to be found along the watercourses, and though too large to be cut down they were in some cases splintered with cannon-ball .Which were fired at the villages in which they grew. Sven in the matter of iron toole these people provided for themselves; they also made implements that were Ndily sold in the markets of Mooch. They obtained iron from the crude ore found in a mine belonging to people of Dalvori, who kept the workieg,of the mine the smelting of the 'ore as a tnonopely hatbeir. awn boucle. The ore, after being laboriously-,,eXtraeted,,by hand, was reduced in rude furnaces whioh,were kept at malting heat by band-bellows day and night, twe [Weeks I at a time. The only fuel used was wood, and-care was' taken not to let tlie metal run out in quantities larger than a blacksmith could easily handle for the making of -a ploughshare, a, scythe, axe, sword, knife, these 'being the objecti oielinarily required. 'lt' has been repoited that theiOstakicksmithe even bad the skill to itteeTii-riflesbarrel,tblit, this is a mistake. The bringing of siOnd and water Iris always a' woman's; .The men, with the help.of their animals, ploughed the fields, sowed and irrigated them, but the hand-reaping, a very slow and laborious task, was done by their wivee and sisters, who also winnowed and cleaned the grain after tlarinen bad thrashed it. The straw was carefully' 'stored for thefoed of the horses and cattle during the winter. • [ I have carefully verified all these details of Sasun lite in conversation with Mr. and Mrs. Bedros, who escaped "hist of the massacre in Sasun, and who are saaantry. -I salted each of them to enumeratie the learitine tasks which fell to their respective seies divier6n—orfaticiu 67790" W a quite amused ;at the eager competition into-which-they at once entered. TIT' reader may decide from the list of tasks which • ee7t , had the best of it. What struck me most was that the ,woman entirely failed to mention in her enumeration the , training and cora of children as one of her a ichtional 'burdens, for of course the men do not trouble themeelyee .in this respect. When I called attention to this over- sight they both of them exclaimed, "The children take Care of themselves!" And so they oltac almost from the Gine that they are put upon the ground. Clothed ifl coarse cloth and dark colours, with unkempt hair and dirty faces, they do not present the attractive appear- ance of children in more favoured; countriee. And it must lse' remembered that the young throughout the Orient generally receive much less attention from theil elderfaisan they do with us. They areloved, but not of tie,ni.te.d ; they are rather taught to be inconepicus etspil;to maintain an air of reverence, especially lniAbeirlathers.. At a very early age the children iainittated , into the employmeats which are to ocetniy their !Pres. There was in Sasun no such thing asr-"the school age," for there were no schools.. Almost the only men who knew bow to read and- write were those connected with the church, andlthey svere.by._no .means_adepts— In the matter of,, numbers, however, it was no uncommon thing to end men who could easily make calculations without the aid of figures. These intelligent highlanders; were not with. out a coeception of the value of education, anti had re- , neatedly within late Yeara tried to start schoola 4n their villages, which were -invariably closed by the Govern- , ment alsoon as they were known ,to exi4. The Morality ' of thepeople in Sinful was of' a -,yeey high atapdard, Wine, made .tiyrthenifielves, waif ,gmoderately used ott festive oeClteirMS, lint'drunkennesft_was a thing almost ' unknown. The mountain women, unlike their sisters of the citieta and the plains, used the. veil not to cover the fate,lrat•to fall as a graceful drapery down the-back. They had the _frank and direct look which we are aceuetbined to see only in' children, and like children, they were .quick to detect and resent 'evil; mien with violence, as the intruder would ii.nd to 'lliags.t,TThey had neither laws .nor courts, but referrieljheir_die- .. —elutes to the head man of the village, from whose word :appeal • was very 'rarely made. This head --man, or " " as-he was • called,- held his office simply by .4l:cosnmon consent of the villagers, not es an hereditary right or a prerogative of wealth, but because Of ----his [ superior character and ability. Their religion concerned 'itself only with the barest essentials. They had no more conception of doctrines or of theology than had the neoplewho listened to the Sermon en the Mount. Their knowledge of Christianity presented rather the aspect of ' a story,the characters of whieh were almost as vivid and Jeri!, ae if they had personally known them. While they felt profound reverence for the Virgin Mary, Christ was 'the object of their worship. Their gratitude and loyalty and love to Him would find expression in brief signifi- cant exclamations, - deep sighs, and sometimes silent tears. Such evidences I have frequently noticed among Armenian peasants as they listened to the reading of the Scriptures or, were engaged in their own devotione. Theif first daily act, as they stepped from their dark. cheerless dwellings to greet the earliest signs of dawn, . wee an act of prayer, accompanied by repeated prostra- tions a.ndthe sign of the Cross. Me. Bedrol is positive 'in declaring that the two hundred villagers who,toward the close of the massacre,fnllowed their priest Hohannes into the Turkish camp at Gelliehseizan and threw them- 'selYes on the . mercy of the commander were offered. their Byes no condition that they would trample npon the crucifix and Efolr Gespels,which were brought from the church altar and thrown at their feet ; but the Priest, in horror, refused to commit this sacrilege—to him more dreadful than death—and every member of his flock,followieg, his example, was forthwith butchered. Before the general slaughter began., one of the prominent men was compelled to kneel on halide and feet, and then was led round by a rope with the priest on his hack [to the great amusement of the mocking Mahometan soldiers. tasked Mr. Bedros how he knew these facts, inasmuch as he was not present and there were no survivors. He said he learned them, after quiet had been restored, and Kurdish eye-witnesses whom he knew, as well as _from some of the Turkish garrison who were still stationed at Gellieheuzan when he returned, and who seemed to take satisfaction in bragging of what they had done. Mr. Bedros himself,as ,an act of piety, recovered the horribly mutilated body of Priest !Inhumes as well as that of Priest Bedros, and eareflilly buried them side by side under the shadow of the Chhich. The thonehta of these poor refugees now in London are continually reverting to those terrible scenes, and the look of inexpressible sadness on their feces only grows more marked with time. I asked them the other day what seemed to them the most remarkable Chine that they had noticed in England. The man quietly replied, "I wonder at the houses, the great buildings, the fields all like gardens, the innumerable multitude of people, their wealth and their churches ; but, most of all, I wonder that with all their greatness and power they did not lift a finger to save us, their poor fellow-Christians. of whose sufferings they have se long been officially informed. eLI7:43M

SMOKELESS POWDER IS MADE.

N.

'The Wood fibre, which it the basis Of Schultze powder, purchased in 41. form resembling coareebtotting paper. eis eleaned and torn up Into abrede until as light and fuffy as cotton wool, and dried. A certaie quantity of this fibre is weighed off and passed into the nitrating house. Into each tank is played a mixture of strong- nitric 'acid and sulphuric acid. While the former of them chemicals enters into the composition he explosive, the latter ie only there for the subsidic47 pair. pose of absorbing the water that is formed in the main chemical process, which begins the moment that the wood fibre is intreduced, After a thorough immersion, the Wood fibre and the nitric acid have formed that important alliance which is responsible - for the explosive properties of Schultze powder. The next series of processes are for the purpose of re- moving every trace of free acid from the nitrated fibre, or nitrolignin as it is termed. The latter substance in its pure state is stable find reliable: It may be stored, heated with reasonable limits of temperature, and in maey 'ways-treated with a good deal of familiarity ; but should there remain the slightest trace of uncombined acid,- many uncertainties arise, and the resulting powder will not satisfy the severe requirement of the authorities. The wet.rasss taken from the nitrating tank is put in a centrifugel machine, where a large proportion of super- litmus acid is removed, and then it is tipped into cold water. After this it has no ,peace for about four weeks —it is boiled, it is torn up and disintegrated by " devilsf" - and rolled under five-ton edge runners. The nitrated fibre is now ready. granting (A-course that it is passed by the chemist in charge, for the second portion of its treatment, viz., its formation into grains. In the first place it is filled into small sacks, and placed under a hydraulic press, from which it emerges compara- tively dry and in the form of a hard cake. After this it receives a further course of edge runners, and the oppor- tunity occurs for adding to the nitrated fibre and other ingredients, of which oxidising salts and paraffin are chiefly notable. The next process is the simple one of shaking it up in sieves with a very fine mesh. This causes the detached pieces of fibre to become granular as a result of the bumping they get against one another; and when grains small enouqh to pass the mesh are formed, they fall through and are collected beneath. A repetition of this process in a sieve with a smaller mesh serves to improve the form and regularity of the grains. The manufacture of Schultze powder is new approaching. completion, for these grains have only to be dried, and then they will resemble this powder as it was prior to the beginning of last year; but now the hardening-pro- cess is added. We need not linger over the drying. , beyond stating that the powder is spread on canves trays and a current of warm air is passed through it until the drying is complete. The grains are hardened by being placed in revolving barrels, kept at a fairly high temperature by hot water jackets' and having poured into them a collodion mixture. The effect of being shaken up in close contact with this mixture,

11,011)E FOR PRODUCING THE MILLENNIUM -

Anthony Hope contributes the first instalment of some delightful gossip under the curious title of "The Fly on the Wheel" to the June number of the " Windsor Magazine." Referring to idle chatter about the Millen- nium, he sari r--" No doubt the Millennium will come some day,but it is certain that several General Elections will be necessary in order to determine what form it is to take. Shall we all do as we like then P This is the natural man's ideal, though Mr. Matthew Arnold was very Contemptuous about it. Or shall we none of us do as everybody else likes P—which seems to be the Socia- list ideal. What is the new state of things to be like ? The poet has painted it

.• There love had never a fear or doubt : December breathed like June : • The Prima Donna ne'er was out Of temper, or of tune.

The st;ean were paved with mutton pies, Potatoes ate like pine, Nothing looked bla^k but woman's eyes. Nothing gTew_old but wine." It sounds very ,pleasant. But where would the poets (novelists are beneath consideration) be without love's doubts and what—say skaters to perpetual summer? Some r;eople don't care for singing even when they are told that it is in tune (they don't know themselves). The vegetarians would agitate to have up the mutton-pies and petition the Vestry (if there were a Vestry then) to lay down cabbages. We should fall ill with pines, the wine would be locked tip, and—well, s good deal of trouble • might arise over the other matter. It has, before now. There is, however, one recipe for prOdue- ing the Millennium which sounds very attractive; The demand is sometimes made nowadays that each sex shottld make laws for itself. I have an amendment to propOse. The two sexes,' it is said in the' Vicar of Wakefi .1c1," seem placed as spies upon e,ach other, and are furnished with different abilities, adapted for mutual inspection.' Very well then let each make laws for the other.. How good men would be, and how pleasant women under such a regime! The result would be grati- fying to both; for men always desire to be good,although they seldom attain the goal, and women are burdened with a natural excellence that stands sometimes most seriously in the, way of their being agreeable."

TEL - WILL PISA.GREEMENT AT SAUEY BRIDGES. _ ANOTSEit CASE IN COURT.

At the Warrington County Petty Sessions, on Wednes- day, before Mayor Pickmere and Mr. Z. Armitage, a case was head which was a sequel to the one heard some weeks ago and which arose out of the division of the late Mr. Hatton's will. On this occasion Ellen Llewellyn was charged with assaulting Alice Atherton on the 27th May. Mr. Davies appeared for the complainant, and Mr. Browne for the defendant.

Mr. Davies Said, in opening the case, that his client, Mrs. Atherton, was a widow living with her brother at 258 Liverpool-rood, and on the 27th May at nine o'clock, in the morning she was going with a bucket of "swill" to her pigs. When passing the daar of her daughter's heuse the defendant came up and without any provoca- tion struck her with her elbow, almost knocking her down. Mrs. Atherton then said "I don't know how you can for shame to do this to an old woman; you have hit the wrong one." Elis (Mr. Davies's) client had lived in Sankey Bridges all her life and had never appeared in any case before. She was a peaceable woman and he hoped that if an unjustifiable assault was proved that the Bench would see their way to teach the defendant not do the lrain again. ' Mrs. Athertm in corroborating her solicitor's state- ment said that she saw the defendant come up Sloop Fold and as they came together the defendant raised her elbow and struck complainant a violent blow on tho fleshy part of her arm, causing It to be discoloured. Mr. Browne Do you say you have never had any trouble with these parties ? Complainant: It is over twelve months since. Mr. Browne : Do you remember six months ago being in this Court ? Complainant: Yes, I have nothing to do with that. Mr. Browne : Last Friday did not you knock de- fendant's little boy over Complainant (indignantly) :' I never saw the boy. I should be very sorry to do such a thing. Mr. Browne: Have you not for some time called these people names - - Mx. Davies here' olijkted as complainant had sworn that she had not spoken to them for' twelve months. The objection was lost sight of and after an excited denial of complainarit,Mr.Browne said : Listen woman, do you ever call out horrible names to this woman (defendant) ? - Complainant (angrily) : Go and ask my character in Sankey Bridges and ask hers. l/dr. Browne : If your tongue ran this way I don't wonder at the assault. Mr. Browne : Did you go to Mr. Dennett, the signal- man and tell him you would get him the sack if he went to his master? OomplainaütF,l salted him if he saw her hit roe. Mr. Davies: Mr. Browne has asked you, a good many questions about what you said, is there any question about you not saying it Complainant: No there is not ; I never said such things. Mary Gasket', wife of John Gaskell, Sankey Brichteq. and daughter of complainant said that she saw defend- ant strike her mother with her elbow just as she was passing her (witness's) house. She never heard ". her mother use foul language, either then, or in her life before. In consequence of the assault her mother's arm went black. . • Mt Browne for the defence said that this case was of old standing and started when Mr. Hatton's money was left to defendant's party, as complainant expeeted tp,be in the will, and she was not. On. Friday complainant knocked his client's little boy down in the street and on the Monday, defendant had walked passed her without touching her. She got off the footpath to do so. Isaac Dennett, signalman on the London and North Western Railway deposed that he saw all the occurrence from his door close by and defendant did not strike com- plainant but the latter used the language mentioned in

Mr. Deollik-I—TtlitlirAtbeton and [Mrs.- Gaskelt-say the defendant struck the former, do you say it is all a lie. Witness : There was no blow struck. The noise of the quarrel drew my attention to it. Mr. Davies : Did von not tell Mrs. Lee there was no blow sfitick as you saw. Witness : Na. Mr. Davies : Then lam sorry for you. How much have you been paid for this evidence. Mr. Browne: He has been paid a shilling and nothing else. He has been given none before. Alice Bough, sister of defendant said that there was no blow struck whatever,and she saw the whole affair. Major Pickmere : Dismissed. The parties then left the Court with apparently mixed feelings.

Fourteen lads belonging to St. Margaret's Bible Class, Rochester, boarded a boat in the Medway at Yalding, near Maidstone, on Monday. The boat was rnly licensed to carry eight, and immediately sank. Mr. W. Lewis, the curate, who was in charge of the party, plunged into the river and succeeded in saving eleven of the lads, but three were drowned.

The Sankey Wire Mills had a ridiculously easy victory on Saturday as they beat Hough Green by five wickets and 63 runs. Seven of the Hough Green men did not score, nor could they obtain a single extra. With the exception of Sparkes, they gave a display which did not even approach mediocrity. Of the six Mill men who batted four, Williams, Marshall, Woodcock and Simpson got double figures. Hobson was the most successful bowler, as he obtained five Hough Green wickets.

-- The Y.M C.A. almost beat Wigan Independent by two to one on Saturday. Excellent fielding by the " Young men," excellent batting by White, and excellent howl- ing by Roddy and Chadwick, contributed to this result. White's batting stands out especially prominent, for he was the only player on his side to achieve double figures, and, therefore, his 34 rather palls the rest.

--- Peter Walker and Sons, both first and second, are proof against all comers, and their latest victims are Penketh Wesleyans and Castle Rubber Works, teepee-

tively. The fit:St team's victory was not quite so pro- nounced as usual, although 68 to 48 is pretty decisive. W. Ashton and J. Bolton with 29 and 23, respectively, gave the brewers a good lead, while H. Barlow and A. Greenhalgh with ten each -were the top scorers for the Penketh team. Although Penketh scored 20 less than their opponents, they obtained six more extras. The second brewery team got the Castle Rubber out for the paltry sum of 14, and then pie; on 76. This leaves no doubt as to which was the better team on Saturday.

Buckley-st. had two matches during the holidays,win- ning the first against Bewsey-road Y.P.S.C.E.,and losing ' the second against Lymm. In the Lymm match two innings were played. Hinchcliffe and Leach bowled well in each match, thellatter in the Lymm match tak- ing seven wickets fqr.nye ,xuns. He also accomplished ' the hat trick. • • • n. In first 9.lass cricket, Gloucestershire on Tlesday sus- tained thir first defeat of the season, and Sussex by beating thistitsgiklitiad their first, victory. W: G. Grace as usual proved the hest man on his aide, but he was not so successful as usual, and his batting average has suffered considerahty in consequence, Hampshire are coming to the fore at a great pace, and in the two matches they have already played, they have beaten 1 Somerset and Derbyshire, the former after having to fol- low on in a large minority. They made a sorry exhibi- tion of Derbyshire ni Monday and Tuesday, for they appeared to have a contempt for the bowling of the Peak men, and then bowled them out for very small totals. It is very probable that they will finish up at 1 the end of the season nearer the top than the bottom. I

Lancashire always get the better el Leicestershire, no matter how well the latter are playing. This has again proved to he the case, and although Leicester have heat.m Surrey and Notts in succession, they were beaten by 177 runs by Lancashire on Saturday. Mold came out at this match in astonishing fashion, and showed that he can hat as well as bowl. He cvnsiderahly as- tonished everyone including himself by scoring 57, and the strange result of the innings was that only the top and bottom plavers got into double figures,and yet Lan- cashire scored 188.

If there is a match which Lancashire would care to win,it is that with Yorkshire, and the annual encounters between the rival " Roses" are always very keenly con- tested struggles. The match which took place on Mon- day, Tuesday, and Wednesday of the present week pro- mised at t`..e commencement to be a very close affair, as the Yorkshire's first innings were only five behind

match in the second inninis, and won by a large majority. Surrey and Notts are also keen rivals, and they like Lancashire and Yorkshire, always play their matches on Whit and August Monday. Surrey have won easily this time, although there has been some good scoring on each side.

have no cause to be ashamed of their past history. The volunteers are really the only portion of the army whick peace men. could defend without sacri- ficing their principles, for the motto of the volun- teers is" Defence not defiance." They have nothing to do with the aggressive policy of the country. , They have only to defend England if foreign armies should invade her shores. liappily there is not much fear at present of such occurring, and it is to be devoutly hoped that the services of the volun- teers will never be required.

The danger of carrying lighted paraffin lamps has often been dilated upon, and another warning to those who indulge in this practice has been given by the sad death of a man named Lash in Warring- ton who dropped the lamp while carrying it upstairs. He was instantly enveloped in flames, and suffered such severe injuries that he succumbed the next day. The members of the jury at the inquest very rightly drew attention to the dangerous practice of carrying lamps, but even the large number of acci- dents which occur from this cause, does not seem to act as a preventative.

The Great Sankey Parish Council has very soon had a reply to the request they made te the District Council to deal with the sewage question. The application on their behalf was made by Mr. T. Halton and the members of the District Council who were getting tired of a rather prolonged meet- ing, with great alacrity's: granted permission to the Parish Council to deal with the matter as they think fit. The Great Sankey Parish Council has now a bit of practical work in front of it. It will, in fact, now have a chance to show how the sanita- tion of a village ought to be done.

-

Some of the Tory newspapers have been greatly nonplussed by the result of the Derby. That Lor Rosebery should again win this great turf event,or, indeed, win anything, was treated as utterly un- reasonable, and on the very day that the race was run the " Globe " delivered itself thusly :—" Only a year ago the victory of Lord Rosebery's Ladas in- spired the Separatists with new hopes and new enthusiasms. Nothing succeeds like success,and it is unquestionable that among the large number of people who owe allegiance to neither party—the men of no settled political opinions, whose voice so often decides the fate of elections—the triumph of Ludas must have exercised an important, if ephe- meral, influence. But the times are sadly changed since the Premier led back his gallant steed amid the plaudits of the multitude! Quantum mutatus ab !" The last sentence (excluding the hack- neyed Latin phrase) of this extract is good. But the " Globe " of the next day tried to be equal to the occasion. It could not dispute the fact that the Premier again led back his gallant steed," but it did deny him "the plaudits of the multitude." This was rather mean, for, according to all other ac- counts, Lord Rosebery received the " plaudits" as well as the victory.

WHIT MONDAY AT EARLESTOWN.

PROCESSION OF SUNDAY SCHOOLS.

For three or four years past, Whit Monday has been recognised in Earlestown as a general holiday, this having to a large extent been brought about by the officials of the various Sunday schools, who decided to hold the annual treats of all the schools cn Whit Mon- day. This, however, only applies to the dissenting bodies, the Church of England people having refused to join in the idea. All the works in the neighbourhood were closed, and the tradesmen also closed their estab- lishments, which enabled those of their employes con- nected with the schools to take part in the festivities. Despite the attractions in the town, a large number of People left by train for various places, principally Liverpool (en route for New Brighton) and Manchester. The morning opened very dull and threatening, and fears were entertained that rain would mar the pleasure of the children. Soon after half-past nine the schools began to muster on the Market-place, and take up their allotted stations. By ten o'clock all were assembled, the Vulcan Wesleyans being led on to the ground by the band of the Salvation Army, the mem- bers of the local corps falling in as they passed near the barracks. When all had assembled hymns Nos. 1 and 2 on the papers distributed were sung, to the accompani- ment of the Earlestown brass band, Mr. T. Clare actiag as conductor. At the conclusion of the singing the schools moved off in the following order, headed by the ' Earlestown band, Mr. J. Taylor officiating as inarshall. ' Behind the band came the ministers of the places of worship represented by the schools, viz., Revs. J. , Bartley (Wesleyan), E. R. Davies (Primitive Methodist), E Miller (Baptist), Captain Horne (Salvation Army). I Then followed the Primitive Methodists, Wesleyans, Sutton-road band, Baptists, Vulcan Wesleyans, Salva- tion Army, Welsh Wesleyans, and nine lorries contain- ing the younger children of each school. After leaving the Market-ground the route traversed was by way of Market-street, Queen.st., Houghton-at., Derby-street ! Hotel, Bridge-st.,Earle-st. to Earle Cottage,returning by; way of Bank-st., Wood-st.,Viaduct-st., Regent-st. to the Market ground, where hymn No. 3 on the paper was sung, and after singing the National Anthem, the children were marched to the various schools and after- wards dispersed. In one or two of the streets there was a display of bunting, and it was admitted on all hands that the procession this year was far ahead of any previous year. Below will be found details of the various schools, and their doings. PRIMITIVE METHODISTS. Banner Messrs. T. Ormand, J. Woods and E. Dickens! 305 in procession, 30 teachers. After the procession they reassembled at the school at 1.30, and marched to a field near the Nine Arches, kindly lent by Mr. Worsley. Here they indulged in games until tea time, tea being served in the field, after which the evening was spent in racing scrambling and numerous games. BRUNSWICK WESLEYANS. After the banner, came Messrs. G. Clarke, W. Pearson W. Clare, T. Clare, and P Parr (secretary). Number in procession 470 including 70 teachers. They reassembled at the field behind Messrs. Vicar's works, lent by Mr. T. Sandersen, at two o'clock, where they amused them- selves until four o'clock, when they proceeded to the schoolroom for tea which was much enjoyed, by all present.

BAPTISTS.

After the banner came Messrs. Hume, Evett and S. Harvey. Number in procession 226 including 20 ;eachers. Reassembled at the school at 1 30, and marched by way of Crow-lane, and through Holly House farm by permission of Mr. Molyneux, to the Earlestown football ground, which has been engaged by the Baptist Cricket and Tennis Club for the season. They arrived at the field about 2.30, and amused themselves until tea was ready, which was served in the field. After tea, games and rscing were indulged in, and nuts and sweets were distributed to the children. A move for home was made about dusk. vOLOAN WESLEYANS. Following the banner were Messrs. P. M. Catnnhell, E. Martin and R. Naylor. Number in procession 213, with n teachers. The children met at the school after din- ner, and marched to a field at Winwick kindly lent by Mr. Kerfoot. Tea was served in the field, and the chil- dren were presented with a bag containing nuts, sweets and an orange. Games were indulged in until dusk.

WELSH WESLEYANS. After the banner came Messrs. J. Morris (superinten- dent), M. Watkins, and J. Williams. Number in pro- cession 88 scholars, and 5 teachers. After dinner they proceeded to a field behind Messrs. Vicar's works, lent by Mr. Saunderson. Tea was served in the field, and the evening was spent in games and scrambling for nuts, - -

SALVATION ARMY. The members the Army were led by the official ban- ner, Captain and Mrs. Howe being in command. Each of the female "soldiers " wore a white sash. Number in procession 88. After dinner they mustered at the barracks, and headed by the band, marched to a field at Burtonwood, lent by Mr. Worsley, where tea was served and the evening was afterwards enjoyably Spent. CHRISTIANS MEETING-ROOM. The members of this place of worship did not take part in the demonstration, but left the town for Frods- ham. They took the 8.45 train aril on arrival at Fredsham at once made their Way to the hills. Tea was partaken of at Mersey View, and the day was spent in an enjoyable manner.

WESTON- POINT.

ACCIDENT TO A Cintn.—The three years' old son of Mr. James Tinsley, lock:nan, Weston Point, sustained somewhat serious injuries on Thursday evening. It appears that the child was on the top of a wall when it fell. Dr. McKenzie was called in, and he found on examination, that the child's thigh was broken.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

SUCCESS OF A WIDNES BAND. At the brass bana contest held in connection with the Llangollen Eisteddfod on Whit-Monday the Widnes Sub- scription Prize Band were awarded the prize of £l5 for the best rendering of the selection " Lohengrin " by Wagner. BOWLING HANDICAP. The finishing stages of a bowling handicap at the Wilson's Hotel, Runcorn, were played on Friday night. The result was that Mr. T. Dean carried off the first prize, Mr. John Heath second, Mr. Charles Hedgcosk third, and Mr. George Mackie fourth. There were 34 entries, and to the entrance fees Mr. Dutton added a copper kettle. ST. LUKE'S SUNDAY SCHOOL.

In connection with this school, anniversary services will be held on Sunday in Bethesda Chapel. The preacher will be the Rev. J. D. Thomas, of Bolton,l who was formerly at Runcorn. The rev, gentleman wil also deliver an address to parents and scholars in St. Luke's Chapel, Pennington-row,at 2.30. The collections will be in aid of the school.

The results of the recent examination for Cheshire County Council scholarships in Class I, open to pupils from elementary schools, kc., and tenable at secondary schools, have been issued. The Altrincham district supplies a large proption of the successful scholars, the only one from the Runcorn district being William Jones, of Weston Point, who has attended the Wesleyan school Runcorn. MILL Beow RAGGED SUNDAY &WOOL.

An entertainment consisting of songs, recitations, and choruses, was given in Bethesda schoolroom on Monday night, and was greatly appreciated by the large number of friends present. The programme was entirely pro- vided by members of the Rev. J. Simpson's children's class. An interesting feature of the proceedings was the presentation of a number of prizes to the children, which were distributed by the pastor. The accompanist during the evening was Mr. C. Le Couteur. At the close of the entertainment the children enjoyed a romp. _

PRIMITIVS METHODIST CAMP MEETING ;T-RIINCORN,.

...—... Special services were held in connection with the Greenway-road Primitive Methodist Chapel, Runcora,on Sunday. In the morning a number of the congregation assembled at Delph Bridge, and marched to the chapel. Various halts were made on the route, and addresses given. Through the inclemency of the weather the ser- vice was held in the chapel, when Mr. Hillidge, Mr. Hayes, and the Rev. H. R. Didcock spoke. In the after- noon they assembled in Devonshire-square, and thence marched to Runcorn Hill, singing hymns on the way. At the open-air servic2a addresses were given by Mr. Charles Hill, Mr. Peacock, Mr. Spruce (Preston Brook), and the Rev. T. Bramall. In the evening there was a large attendance at the chapel, when a sermon was preached by Mr. John Wilson, after which a love feast was held. Ste J. T. BRUNNER, BLIZT., M.T. .

The following acknowledgment has been received by the Rev. J. Simpson, secretary of the Runcorn Noncon- formist Ministers' Association,in response te the letter of congratulation to Sir J. T. Brunner, Bart., M.P., on re- ceiving his baronetcy. The letter is forwarded from 9, Ennismore Gardens, London, and the text of it is as follows :—" Please present to your colleagues and accept for yourself my cordial thanks for your con- gratulations and more especially for.the very kindly terma of your letter. It is my sincere hope that this new distinction which has been conferred upon me may be a help and not a hindrance to my work; the incentive to better things and not a temptation to abandon the principles I learnt from my father and have hitherto been guided by. With kind regard and renewed thanks to you and to your reverend brethren, I remain, yours faithfully, John T. Brunner."

RUNCORN PoLics Nnws.

At Runcorn Podce Court on Thursday, before Mr. P. Speakman, C.C., and Mr. E. Imison, Richard William Price, aged 10, living with his parents at 10, Surrey-st., was charged with stealing a purse containing 71d. in money and two silver studs from the person of Maud Kirkham, daughter of Mr. G. H. Kirkham, greengrocer, High-st.,Runcorn, on Tuesday.—Maud Kirkham stated that her attention was called to the defendant by another child, who told her that she had seen him put his hand in her pocket. She then found that she had lost her purse, containing Md. and two silver studs. She in- formed the police.—Police Constable Gregory deposed to taking the defendant into custody who told him that he had spent the money and had given the studs to some boys on the fair ground. He afterwards succeeded in tracing the purse and studs and returned them to the owner.—Superintendent Atherton stated the boy had a bad character, and had been convicted of pocket picking before.—The Bench sentenced him to receive six strokes with the birch red.—Evan Roberts, a sailor, was appre- hended by Police Constable Gregory for being drunk and disorderly in Irwell-st. on Monday evening, and was fined ss. including costs.—Margaret Dalton, for being drunk in Lock-st. at six o'clock on Thursday morning, was fined ss. including costs. P.C. Wylie proved the case. SAD DROWNING FATALITY AT Rli/NCORN. This (Friday) morning at 3.15, the body of a boy eight years of age, named Ralph Ravenscroft, who re- sided with his parents at 6, Lyon-street, Runcorn, was found floating in the Bridgewater Canal at Bate's Bridge, Runcorn. He had been miss!.ng from his home since Monday morning when it appears that he was sent to a neighbouring shop by his mother. He was only partly dressed at the time, wearing trousers, laced-up boots, and shirt. As he did not return home enquiries were made, and it was ascertained that h,, had been seen during the day on his father's boat" Bertie "which was found lying in the middle basin of the old dock. Grap- pling operations were carried on during the week. The body was picked up by George Pickstuck of the flat " Herbert" belonging to the Manchester Ship Canal Company. Itwas brought to Top Locks, Runcorn, and afterwards:conveyed home. The Coroner has been com- municated with, and an inquest will be held in due course. RUNCORN VOLUNTEER SHOOTING CLUB. The fifth gold medal competition for this season took place on Saturday at Runcorn Heath. To start with the climatic conditions were nearly all that could be desired, but at the close of the competition, at 600 yards, a drenching rain descended and obscured the light,render- ing good scoring next to impossible. The Burnley Town Council at its meeting on Wednes- day adopted a new by-law framed with the object of putting an end to the practice of betting in the streets,a practice which the police have hitherto been unable to suppress. The new by-law provides thatanyone found betting in the public thoroughfares shall be liable to a tine not exceeding £5.

THE EXAMINER. SATURPAY, JUNE 8 1895

WHITSUNTIDE AT RUNCORN. THE PROCESSIONS AND EXCURSIONS.

Whit Monday is always a red letter day for Run- cornians,and this year it proved no exception to the rule. The march out of members of friendly societies occurred in the morning, and was followed later on in the day by the annual Sunday schocil procession. For the success of the fete fine weather is important in the highest degree, and in this respect all were favoured on Monday, for the weather, though threatening at times, was fine throughout the day. The streets presented a gay and festive appearance. There was a good display of bunting, many of the banners protruding from the windows of shops and houses. Littletchange had been effected in the arrangements of previonQ years. Shortly after ten o'clock two of the friendly' societies paraded the principal streets of the town and thence proceeded to Halton. The societies were the Star of Freedom and the Lily of the Valley— both connected with the Grand United Order of , Oddfellows. They were accompanied by bands,and the banners of the lodges were held aloft. The Suaday school procession was as usual composed of two sections. The children attending the Sunday schools of the Church of England were the first to wa!k in procession and they were followed half-an- hour later by the Nonconformists who were by far the more numerous. Notwithstanding thecrowded state of the somewhat narrow streets no difficulties were experienced by t processionists, which was largely due to the excellent arrangements made by the police which were carried out under the direc- tion of Superintendent Atherton, Inspector Hicks, and Sergeant Brewster. Appended will be found full r rticulars rer -cting both processions.

par. ospe. pr 'THE FRIENDLY SOCIETIES PROCESSION. As already mentioned, two friendly societies only walked in procession, viz :—The Lily of the Valley Lodge, No. 515 ; and the Star of Freedom Lodge, both of which are connected with the Graed United Order of Oddfellows. Assembling at their respective lodge rooms, the two societies proceeded to Devonshire-square, and from here they departed in processional order, taking the following route—High-street, Bridge-street, Halton- road, to Halton where a short stay was made. The Star of Freedom Lodge, which comprised 92 adults and 108 juveniles, was headed by the Runcorn Volunteer Band which was under the conductorship of Bandmaster Outhwaite. Amongst those in the procession there were: Dr. Robinson (medical officer), grand master Findlow, district master William Ling, G. flazlehurst and Mather (trustees), and A. Gregory (auditor). Bro. W. B. Jones (secretary) was unable to be present as he was attending the A.M.C. of the Oraer at Newcastle. The juvenile members were accompanied by the Mill Brow Drum and Fife Band. New regalia was worn by the officers. With regard to this lodge afew particulars may prove interest- ing. Since the last demonstration two years ago the membership has increased by 44 and the funds by £220. Fourteen new members have been enrolled. whose aver- age age is 17 years. The balance sheet for last year showed that the amount in hand on the sick and funeral fund, on the 31st December, 1893, was £560. The con- tributions realised £282, the interest £27, which with sundries made the income into £873 4s. 61. There had been paid for sickness £l4B 15s. 6d., and for deaths £63 Bs., leaving in hand a balance of £661. The number of members is 271, the average age 31 years and nine months , the gain for the year was £lOB, and the worth per member £3 lie. 21d. With regard to the Lily of the Valley Lodge, the members were preceded In the proces- sion by Gossages'(Widnes) Prize Brass Band. There were about 80 members present including :—Dr. McDodgall (medical officer), Bros. H. Holt (president), William Flodgetts, Walter Worthington and T. Nicholls (trus- tees), William Littler (treasurer),William Ford (steward), J. Holt (secretary), and R. Evans (assistant secretary). The proceSsionists returned to Runcorn about twelve o'clock. With reference to this lodge it may be stated that in the year 1894, it commenced with a membership of 374. Ten were admitted ; and nine re- signed membership ; thus leaving the number of mem- bers at the close of the year at 375. The contributions brought in £383 lie., the interest on capital £Bl 13s. 4d. received from district .£5O, and sundry receipts made a total of £2,134 3s. 6d. There had been paid for sick £224 35., district funeral fund £93 4s. 441., in death claims £6O, and sundries made the total disbursements £387 lie. 84d.The management fund commenced with a balance of £144, and ended with one of £l6B. The assets of the Lodge are £1,956 2s. 54d., of which £1,400 is in- vested in freehold property. THE SUNDAY SCHOOL SCHOLARS' PROCESSION. About mid-day the Church of England Sunday school scholars assembled at their respective schools, where they were formed into processional order and proceeded to Church-street to join the Parish Church S4holars. The procession was distinct trom that of the Nonconformists and the route taken was from Church-street along Bridge street, Victoria-road to the Vicarage. The

PARISH CHURCH SCHOOL scholars marched in advance with the large banner, and the Runcorn Volunteer Band at their head. This contin- gent numbered about 800 and amongst those present were the Vicar (Rev. Canon Maitland Wood), Rev. H. B. Firth, Messrs. E. Imison, J.P., John Littler, E. Key, J. Yates, Frankland, Bowyer, Garner, Gibson, Tonks, Hulme, W. Dudley, W. E. Dudley, and Clare. The marshall was Mr. W. E. Dudley. On arrival at the vicarage they were entertained by Mr. and Mrs. Woods, and afterwards proceeded to a field adjoining, lent by Mr. William Bankes where games were indulged in until tea. This was served in the Parish Church sehools. An entertainment followed.

HOLY TRINITY SCHOOLS with the Mill Brow Ragged School and those connected with the Mariners' Church followed next in procession. The number of scholars was as follows :—Holy Trinity, 200 ; Mill Brow Ragged School, 120 ; Mariners' Church, 26. They were headed by the Mill Brow Drum and Fife Band, and amongst those present were :—The Rev. Dr. Preston (vicar), Rev. A. H. Bartram (curate), Messrs. Hulse, Kilcross, 'turner, ilampson, Woods, Prince, Pear- son, D. Smith, Broughton, Finch, and E. R. Westbrook (secretary). The field to which the scholars afterwards repaired was lent by Mr. Hulse, and was near the foot- ball field. A tea and entertainment was afterwards provided in Trinity schools. S. MICHAEL'S." . . .

In addition to the Sunday school scholars connected with S. Michael's, there walked in the procession mem- bers of the Mothers' Meeting Class and several of the congregation. The scholars numbered 138 boys, 120 girls, and 48 infants. A large banner was at the head of the procession, and immediately behind were the Rev.A. J. Jameson, Messrs. W. Mellor, Williams, W. Cropper, Sherlock, C. Pownall, Clarke, Blundell, and Ridge, with Messrs. W. J. Austin and Cooke (secretaries). The scholars enjoyed a romp in a field near the Vicarage for a few hours and then proceeeded to Shaw-street schools for tea.

===== 0002601_18950608_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
MERSEY MISSION TO SEAMEN.

The rear of the procession was brought up by those connected with the Runcarn branch of the Mersey Mission to Seamen,who were accompanied by their drum and fife band which was under the conductorship of Mr. R. Clays. Included in the procession were: Mr. W. Shaw (missionary), Captain Crowther, Mr. W. Byfield, Mr. W. Shaw, jun., several teachers in connection with the Ellesmere Port branch, and about 300 scholars with their teachers. The infant scholars were conveyed in two vehicles kindly lent by Mr. R. Abel, and the Man- chester Ship Canal Company. After a short stay at the Vicarage they wended their way to the Rope Walk field lent by Mr. R. Abel where races and games were held.

===== 0002601_18950608_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NONCONFORMIST FROCEbSION._

—..—.-- The scholars of the Nonconformist and Catholic schools formed one united procession. Twelve schools were represented and the total number who walked was 3,240. This"was a slight falling off as compared with last year when the figures were 3,308, which was the larg- est number since 1890. All the processionists adjourned to one field—The Runcorn Football field. Games were engaged in for a short time, and then the scholars proceeded to their respective schools where tea was partaken of. The general marshals were Messrs. R. W. Harrop and J. Lunt; rdr. J. Halford performed the duties of general secretary, and to these gentlemen much credit is due for their excellent arrangements. The route taken was from Church-street along Waterloo- road, High-street, Bridge-street, Halton-road,and thence by way of Canal-street, and Wivern-place to the Foot- ball Field. The order of procession was determined by the schools which were established in the town, the old- est established being at the head. This honour fell to the

BRUNSWICK-STREET SCHOLARS who numbered 597. Immediately behind their large banner there were: The Rev. T. Brackenbury ksuper- intendent), Messrs. John Simpson, J.Bowyer, R.Rawlin- soo, B. WESTON.

_ The Sunday school scholars at Weston had their annual treat on Monday. Those connected with the Wesleyan schools formed in procession and walked through the streets and lanes of the village, making halts at several residences. They were preceded by their own brass band, and at the head of the procession, immedi• ately behind the large banner, there walked the three superintendents Messrs. James Ball, William Deinbabin and James Ford, wi'h Mr. John Ball and Mr. F. Fitton (secretaries). Abeut 300 were in the procession including the teachers and members of the congregation. A num- ber of infants were conveyed in a lurry which had been nicely decorated. Tea was served in the schoolroom. An adjournment was afterwards made to one of Mr. William Dunbabin's fields where races and sports were engaged in.—Those connected with the Cnurch schools after a short service, walked in procession to the Vicar- age, Runcorn. They were headed by the Weston Church Band, and in addition to the Sunday school scholars, there were about forty lady meinb:q.s of the congrega- tion and about the same nntubor connected with the Girl's Friendly Society present. The Rev. W. H. Stables with Mr. J. Ford, jun., and Mr. O. Robinson (wardens), and Messrs. J. Mather, J. Bankes, and T. Ford, jun. (sidesmen), also walked in the procession. The scholars numbered nearly 300. The choir boys were in Eton dress, and the infant scholars were conveyed in lurries decorated for the occasion and lent by Mr. T. Bankes and Mr. F. Ford. The procession was a most attrac- tive one, and presented a pleasing sight as it marched round the lawn in front of the Vicarage. On a halt being made, the scholars and friends partook of the hospita- lity of Mr. and Mrs. Wood. Mr. F. Ford, Mr. G. Robin- son, and Mr. W. Moores marshalled the procession. The returr. journey to Weston was shortly afterwards com- menced, when on arrival at the schools about 5.30, the scholars were further regaled.

===== 0002601_18950608_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXCURSIONS.

The London and North-Western Railway Company provided an attractive bill of fare for Whitsuntide, and many took advantage of the cheap means of locomotion offered. On Saturday a good number booked for watering places on the North Wales coast, and returned home on Tuesday and Wednesday. Tuesday, however, was the busiest day for the employes at Runcorn station as may bp jud.ged from the following figures :—For Chester, 300 • Frodsham, 900 ; North Wales,4oo ; Liver- pool, 1, ' 000 ; Longsight (Mr Belle Vue) 200. The ex- cursionists to Chester consisted for the most part of members of the Bethesda Congregation Christian En- deavour class and their friends who were accom- panied by the Pastor (Rev. J. Simpson). This contingent spent the morning in Chester, and about 80 afterwards proeeeded in a steam launch to Eccleston Ferry. The Duke of Westminster's park was visited, and some were conducted through the hall. Tea was partaken of at Eceleston, after which the party journeyed to Chester again. They sauntered about the town until train-time when the return journey was commenced, Runcorn being reached about nine o'clock. The arrangements, which were very satisfactory, were made by Mr. W. Lacy and Mr. P. Cartwright.—ln addi- tion to the excursions by rail, two trips down the Mei, sey to Liverpool and New Brighton were arranged.E irly on Tuesday morning hundreds of people were to be seen making their way to the Bottom Locks whence the two steamers departed. The excursion promoted by Mr. William Shaw, missionary of the Mersey Mission to Sea- men (Runcorn Branch).was well patronised The steamer engaged was the Crocus, belonging to the Wallasey Ferry Company. Over 700 persons embarked, and at 9.30, the vessel steamed down the Ship Canal to Weston Point locks, and thence by the river Mersey to Liverpool. A number of passengers broke their jour- ney at this point, whilst the remainder proceeded to New Brighton. Visits were paid to numerous places of interest; the excursionists being admitted free to the Sailors Home, Liverpool, and at a reduced rate to the Palace, New Brighton. The return journey was made from New Brighton a little after 7.30. The band of the Seamen's Mission was in attendance and played lively tunes. Runcorn was safely reached about ten o'clock, all having had a most enjoy- I able outing. The weather was gloriously fine, and there was no accident of any kind to mar the enjoyment of the excursionists,—About 500 persons joined the excur- sion in connection with Weston Wesleyan Sunday school. The saloon steamer, Daniel Adamson, of Manchester,was engaged and it departed from the Pier Head, Bottom Locks, shortly after nine o'clock. It took the same course as that taken by the Crocus. The return journey was completed about nine o'clock. The trip was a most pleasant one and excellent arrangements had been made by Messrs. James and John Ball. 4IP

===== 0002601_18950608_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIZE DISTRIBUTION AT RUNCORN PARISH SUNDAY SOHOOL.

On Whit Sunday, prizes were distributed to the scholars at the Parish Church Sunday schools, who by regular attendance and good conduct during the preced- ing twelve months, were entitled to them. Mr. E. Imison, J.P.. distributed the awards. In doing so he said the conneation which he and his family had had with the schools extended over very many vears,and his presence there that day was an indication that the interest so actively shown by his late father, who was at one time superintendent of the boys' school, was in some ' degree being maintained by himself. The importance of the work being carried on in Church Sunday schools could not be over-estimati d in face of the desire on the part of many people for the secularisation of day school education. It was most gratitying to see that the Parish Church schools were making such steady progress, not only in the increased attendance of scholars, but he had also reason to believe in the efficiency of its teachers. He hoped that those befere him might be a source of strength and well-being not only to their church but also as citizens. Prizes, tor the most part consisting of bibles and prayer books,were then distributed to the following:— Boys school i—Leohard Goldsmith, Charles Bradshaw, Cato Bradshaw„lohn Howard, George Goldsmith, Samuel Clarke, Thomas William Oleave,Josepti Harrison Frank Bradshaw, Edwin Roach, John Hitchmough, Joseph Williamson, Thomas James Clarke, Arthur Yates, Ralph Gibbs, William Corker, William Hill, Walter Gregory,William Ho ward,William Bratt,Walter Mayhew, Herbert Roach, William H. RUNCORN URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL

At the Town Hall, Runcorn, on Tuesday morning, the monthly meeting of the Council was held under the pre- sidency of Mr. E. Imison, J.P. There were present : Rev. Dr. Gastaldi, Dr. McKenzie, Messrs. J. Pritchard, H. J. Grice, G. H. Kirkham,J. W. Lightburn,G.Hewitt, T. Dean, R. H. Posnett, D. Bisbrown M. Sweetman,and J. Wilson, Mr. F. A. Lake (lew clerk), Mr. E. Marshall (secretary), Dr. McDougall (medical officer), Mr. M. Barker (surveyor).

===== 0002601_18950608_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
TiE MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT.

Dr. McDougall reported that the number of deaths registered during the past month aniounted to 20 ; 5 were due to bronchitis, 2 to cancer, 2 to pneumonia, 2 to premature birth,and 1 coach to jaundice,heart disease, mesenteric disease, diphtheria, dropsy, apoplexy, skin disease, phthisis, and enteric fever. The above 20 deaths gave an average death-rate for the 12 months of 12.6. 55 births were registered during the month. Mr. Light- burn said he thought the report contrasted favourably with previous statements. The report was adopted.

===== 0002601_18950608_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
HIGHWAYS AND BUILDINGS COMMITTEE:

This committee recommended that the west side of Station-road be flagged with granolithic flags, and that the tender of the Croft Granite Company to supply and lay the flags in respect of such footway be accepted ; that the amended plan of the house to be erected by Mr. Thomas Handley in Weston-road be approved; that the plan of Messrs. W. Hallding and J. E. Warburton of two semi-detached villas proposed to be built in Norman-road be approved, subject to the cesspool being constructed according to bye-laws ; that the plan of the exors. of the late John J. waterworth for the conversion of two houses at the corner of Greenway-road and Victoria-road into one house and shop with the addition of scullery and larder and the elongation of the bay window into a square shop window, be approved ; that the plan of Mr. John Hudson of four cottages proposed to be built in Dale-street be approved ' • thot the plans of Mr. Gregory for alterations at 9, Wellington-street, and of Mr. W. Hedley,butcher,for alterations at his shop in Greenway- road be disapproved.—The minutes were passed on the motion of Mr. Bisbrown, seconded by Dr. Gastaldi.

HEALTH COMMITTEE.

It was recommended by this committee that the plan of alterations to be made at rear of premises at 29 and 31, High-street be approved. It was also recommended that as a nuisatice was reported by the sanitary inspec- tors to be existing at premises in Milstone-yard, off Mill-st., certain alterations be effected ; that in the event of non-compliance with the notice, the Council de the required work and charge the owner with the cost. —ln the absence of the Chairman of the Health Com- mittee (Mr. Wiswall) who is sojourning in the Isle of Man, Mr. (Mee moved the adoption of the minutes.— Mr. Kirkham seconded the proposition, which was carried.

THE TECHNICAL INSTITUTE.

- The Technical Instruction Committee recommended that as regards the session 1895-6, the teachers be en- gaged on the following terms :—The whole grant on the result of examination, half fees and a capitation allow- ance of 10s. for every student who has qualified for ex- amination by attendance at 20 lessons of one hour's duration, with a minimum payment of £ll and railway fare (including grant earned). In case of teachers resid- ing in or near Runcorn, £lo.—That the Committee be atillhorized to engage teachers on the foregoing terms. When the subjects, however, are such that no grants are received thereon, special terms be arranged ; that a syllabus be prepared by the Secretary; that prizes be given to successful students, and that a sum of £l5 be devoted to such purposes; that the tender of Mr. Thomas Bradshaw for the making and fixing of a new entrance gate to the Institute be accepted; that the tender of Mr. Thomas Caygill for the making and fixing of blinds ,be accepted; that the Committee be empowered to make arrangements for the formal opening of the In- stitute.—Mr. Wilson in moving the adoption of this Committee's minutes, said the Committee had taken the liberty to invite Sir John T. Brunner, Bait., to open the Institute.—Mr. Bisbrown : Have you fixed a day ?—Mr. Wilson : July 17th.—It was explained that Sir J.T. Brunner had written stating that he would be pleased to open the Institute en that date.—Mr. Lightburn said be had great pleasure in seconding the adoption of the recommendations of the committee. The arrangements regarding teachers were for the forth coming session only. He hoped the time might soon come when they would have a different teaching staff:on rather a different basis than at present. He thought they could form a higher grade school in the town, and thus prevent people from going to Liverpool and else- where for such education. A new order had come down to them and with it he thought they might eventually be able to use the large room of the Technical Institute for the purpose of a higher grade day school. Of course, there were difficulties in the way, but he thought it would be a very great advantage to the people of Run- corn. It would give pupils an opportunity of continuing their education at Runcorn, and would relieve them of the expenses of travelling, &c., to other towns. If they could get a teacher—a man of standing, connected with the Institute, he could teach these higher grade subjects at night. It was a matter rather premature at present, but one, he thought they should not lose sight of.—The minutes were passed.

THE ALKALI _COMPANY AND THE COUNCIL.

The Secretary rerd the comrnunicatic-n-i;celved from Mr. Carey, secretary to the United Alkali Company, with reference to the memorial of the Council,which has already been published in the "Examiner."—The Chair- moved that the secretary on behalf of the Council acknowledge the receipt of the corninunication,and state that they hope that when trade does revive, Runcorn may be allowed to have a good share of the prosperity. —Mr. Pritchard seconded the proposition, and said he had riot the least doubt that the Council's efforts had had the result of removing the general impression which prevailed in the town that some of the Company's works at Runcorn were to Ise totally closed. The letter read, set at rest any rumours of that kind. With a revival of trade they would have an increased output at the works in Runcorn, and also there would be a prob- ability of a new development by the Company in the town. It was pointed out by the deputation that Run- corn was as advantageously situated as any of the other places for their works. It was on the banks of the Ship Canal which was not the Case in the days of the cild private concerns, and thus for manufacturing purposes was better situated than ever it was before. When the Company looked into that matter they would find that Runcorn possessed as many advantages as any other place.—The proposition was carried.

===== 0002601_18950608_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
THE UPPER MERSEY NAVIGATION RATES.

Mr. liewitt moved "That Mr. Taylor be requested to give the Council some information respecting the rates charted by the Upper Mersey Navigation Commission as it is felt there is a grievance inasmuch as a higher rate is charged for ships coming to Runcorn than those going to Manchester."—Re said it would be better if the mat- ter were discussed in committee and after some discus- sion it was decided that the subject be referred to the Parliamentary aria General Purposes Committee.

===== 0002601_18950608_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
SANITARY INSPECTOR.

Mr. Posnett on behalf of Mr. Wiswall gave notice that at the next meeting of the Council he would move that Mr. James Percival be re-appointed sanitary inspector for another year.

THE FERRY.

Dr. Gastaldi ask.id whether anything had been done with reference to the ferry. He found that the same difficulties existed. People were still charged for cross- ing the canal,and there was an irregularity in the work- ing of it, some people being unable to cross the water- way when they wished to do so. He thought for the benefit of the community they should know what was being done in the matter.—Mr. Posnett said the Committee which was appointed went over to Widnes b, discuss the matter with the Ship Canal officials; but they had not been able to fix a date for doing so with the London and North-Western Railway Company's officials. So far as concerned the levying of a toll by the Ship Canal Company their solicitors stated that they knew nothing about it, and that they would give information on the subject within the next few days. With regard to the other matters discussed, so far as approaches were concerned, they received very satisfactory assurances from the solicitors that they would be attended to.—Dr. Gastaldi thought they should press forward in the matter, and he suggested that the Clerk be instructed to write to the partiesconcerned, for the purpose of fixing an early date to discuss the matter in order to come to some understanding regarding it.— The Law Clerk said it was not a question of letter writ- ing. Proceedings were pending fur an order to re-instate the ferry and the keepin, of it cpen in the future. The order would have been asked for at the last Assizes but the t wo companies concerned stated that' they had not had time to prepare their defence. The proposed inter- view was to see if some arrangement could not be come to, but if not the order would be asked for at the next Assiz,s. People could refuse to pay the fee charged by the Ship Canal Company.—Dr. Gastaldi hoped it would

go to the public that they were not obliged to pay any- thing for crossing the canal.—The subject then dropped.

THE PRICE OF .AS.

Mr. Lightburn said he would like to know the result of . the deputation to the Runcorn Gas Company. (Laugh- ter.)—Dr. Gastaldi said the deputation appointed did not act, and he had laid himself under a liability of getting another committee. He was determined to go on with the matter. The price of gas was too high. They should either have it reduced, or go in for electric light.—. Nothing further was done in the matter.

A MATTER FOR THE HIGHWAYS COMMITTEE. 1 Mr. Posnett drew the attention of the Council to the fact that a new gate with a padlock had been fixed on a right of way leading to Bate's Bridge. It would be a great inconvenience to many and he moved that it be an instruction to the Highway's Committee to consider the matter. Mr. Hewitt thought that Mr. Posnett would find that there had been a gate there formerly.—The Chairman said he was sure the committee would do wl4t - they could to re-open the old right of way.—The matter was referred to the committee, and the Council then rose.

.LYMAI.

POLICE CASES.-At the Police Court, on Saturday, be- fore Mr. 'F. P. Norris, John Lee and Annie Lemma and wife, were charged with having been found begging at 3.30 p.tn.on the previous day at Warburton. P.H.lhrar- burton proved the case. Defendants were each sent to. prison for seven days.—Cti Monday, before Messrs. A - Watkin and J. Gregson, Henry Bravmdale was charged' with having been drunk and disorderly at Lytrim on the previous Saturday. Acting-Sergeant Washington said that defendant got off a train from Liverpool on the day in question, and on leaving the station he commenced running at and annoying people in the streets. Defend-- ant was fined 5. including costs.

We have just been looking through a copy of " Home Notes." the penny ladies' paper brought out by Mr. C. Arthur Pearson, of " Pearson's Weekly," and we must say that we have been particularly struck with its bright appearance and contents. It is just the ideal size for a borne paper, and, so far as is possible for the masculine mind to judge,- the contents in every department of home work seem eminently practical. " Home Notes" is published every Thursday, and from what we hear enjoys a very large circulation.

AN ARNY DESERTER.—Benjamin Davies, (18), was charged with being a deserter from the Royal Welsh Fusiliers, stationed at Salford, since the 2nd June.—P.C. Smith proved arresting defendant, who was wearing military clothing. Defendant was remanded to await an escort to Strangeways Gaol, and the officer was granted the usual reward. OVERCROWDED AND UNREGISTERED LODGINd-FIGTISES.— The following persons were prosecuted by Nuisance Inspector Norman, for overcrowding their lodging-houses or keeping lodgers without registration. Assistant Inspector Powell gave evidence in each of the cases : James Tandy, 2 liere-st., was fined ss. and costs for having 11 persons in his house, which was registered for sir, on May 29; John Walsh, 8 Jolley's Yard, Friars Green, fined ss. including costs, for keeping lodgers without being registered on 20th May. There were seven in a house adapted for three; Martha Warburton Antrobus-st., same offence and fined, nine persons in' stead of four; John Stapleton, 11 Gee st., same offenc - and date, fined ss. and costs, 12 persona instead of six°

' Si' ' INTERNATIONAL MINERS' *ash. CONFE RENCE. Tqcf,tt,, The-sixth International Miners' Conference, which is tieing held in Paris, was opened on Monday, Mr. T. Ault, 11,1. P., presiding. The forenoon was occupied with the appointment of cotninitteeS and officers, and the afternoon was spent as a holiday. The Conference bad before it on Tuesday a resolution, brought forward by French delegates, condemning over- production as the chief cause of the distress among -miners, and declaring that it was necessary that an in- ternational committee should be formed to regulate pro- :duction. An amendment was moved by British delegates 'recognising the difficulty of the subject and suggesting that it should be referred to the international committee ;with a view- to its being-dealt with at the next Congress. A German delegate spoke strongly against combination rwith the coalowners for the purpose of limiting produc. tion, and was warmly applauded by British and other. delegates. The discussion was at times rather stormy. ,It had not concluded when, the.bour far, adjournment arrived. At Wednesday's sittliii-oTtlieetarigrene Mr. J. Wilson. spoke in support of lie 'amendment to the French, resolution ref the liriltatimi of production. Its propeliaT ,was to refeethe matter toi an internatiehal committee, with a view to its receiving the fullest consideration. He ridiculed M. ,Lesey's scheme as impracticable, and pointed out thettin raising the 'price of coal they would be raising the Roe of every arficle in the production of which coal wak .used. The British and German dele- gates voted " aolid " for Mr. Wilson's amendment, and the French and Belgian delegates for the Freneh resolu- tion, the, result tieing that the amendment was declared carried by 757,300 to 212,000. The eight hours question Wee mitdef:piscussion during the rest of the sitting.

U. 0., of Odd- fellows' Friendly Society, held at the Joiners' Arms Inn, Croft, for the year ending December 31st last, 'LB9 been issued to the members. It appears that the ;society, though only a small one, is in a good ; -financial condition.: The balance in the treasureea" (Mr.. P. J. Bate) and sub-treasurer's ( Nlr. S. Dtivin'a) hands at the beginning of last year was £35 115.10111,, and the receipts for contributions, flees, &s.,- during . the year were £27 Is. 41. The expenditure for funerals was £22, sick pay. £9 45., and for salaries, •rent of rooms, &c., £5 OA: Sid„ leaving a balance in the treasurer's and sub-treasurer's hands, at the end of the year of £25 81, 6d. The amount at the end of the year in the: Warrington Savings Bank was .£395 13s. 9,1., whlaliadded' to the amount in the treasurer's hands iae he total funds at that time .£422 2s 3d Tnç nnjOer Of members in the Society at the end of the.y,ear Was 33. The auditors .for tbe year were. Messrs, Alfred Dixon and Peter Ingham, Mr. Joseph Daintith being. the secretary.

' z•-:' The annual cnnvention of the Irish National League of Great Britain WA* held at Leeds nri'Satiirday., under the presidency of Mr. T. P. O'Connor.M.P., A resolution was passed expiessinglndigeation at the proceedings whioh will shortly causetheretirement of Mr. W. O'Brien from Parliamentary life. 'Mr:Justin Mrarthy,• M.P.' made a speeCh dealing with the question of unity in the 'lrish , Parliamentary party.- ,In the name' of free diacUssion and patriotic feeling gross attacks. Were now being made, on the public and private' Characters of members of the; National party. But in spite of ,calumny be felt justified: in speaking in terms of satisfaction and , hope of the in., ternal history of the party. ' Speaking it a dinner held in the evening, the Hon. E. Blake,' M.P., expressed his strong belief in the permanence and' sincerity of the Liberal and Nationalist alliance.. A great public demon- stration in connection with the convention was held on Tuesday, and addressed by Mr. T. P. O'Connor, M.P., Mr. Justin M'Carthy, M.P., and Mr.- ' John Dillon, M.P.

A party of Nottingham Liberals visited Hawarden on Monday to present Mrs. Gladstone with some beautiful specimens of Nottingham lace. Mr. Gladstone, who was confined to the house with a slight cold, was unable to be present at the nrsgentation, which took place on the terrace. The Dean of Lincoln said that Mr. Glad- stone was suffering from a slight illness, and they were taking great care of him so that he might be able to go on a cruise in Sir Donald Currie's yacht in ten days' time.

A Bristol and London commercial traveller named Richard Sharpe, who had for several days been staying at the Woodside Hotel, Birkenhead, was on Wednesday found to have committed- suicide during the night by shooting himself through the head with 4 revolver.
