In concluditigi,-,:/rit;:'Brewrie In •• observed' it defendant's house was h.fftill6-houee. and beeitivar drunk on the.premises. ' The dog had been in the house I aleny times, and no objection had been taken to it. He, therefore, contended that the plaintiff ;had a right to 'obtain compensation for the loss of the dog which had ' come hy its death owing to being poisoned in the house. , James .Childs,the son of the plaintiff, gave evidence in support of Mr. Browne's statement.--CrOsa-examihed : He said that the dog had stolen a leg of mutton six months ago from the landlord. • Mr. Browne : Is the dog toni,ie poisoned because it stoleo. leg of mutton ? His Eienour : Fortunately the leg of mutton was not poisoned. (Laughter.) . Answering further questions, the witness said that he had not heard of defeudant having prohibited his father or anyone else taking dogs into the house in consequence of the leg of mutton episode. They did not possess the dog's pedigree, nor did they keep it for breeding pur- poses but as .a yard d0g.... .. ..4. ....,..__.... __. Richard Childs, another son ,of defendant, estimate d the value of the dog at £5O. 'lleltilew the dog had been in the Green Man several timellsioce the leg of mutton had been stolen, and no objection had been taken to it. William Childs, husband of the plaintiff, denied that defendant had refused him the house when accompanied by the dog. In reply to Mr. White, be said that the dog when a little puppy, was given to his wife by a gentle- man named Clare who said that the dog was off h very good breed. He had found out that it was a thief, and he therefore never took it out without a slip. John Williams, 67, Lovely-lane, iron worker, said that he had often visited the "Green Man,"and had seen Childs' dog there. No objection had been taken to cus- tomer's dogs coming into the house. Mr. White: Do you know if there is any accommod- ation in the house for dogs? Witness (smiling) : No, I don't know that there is Mr. White. Mr. White,: There is no refreshment, I suppose, for dogs. (Laughter.) Witness: I don't think that there is, but there is plenty of it for men. (Renewed laughter.) The Judge (to Mr. White) : I suppose you will inquire .next, whether there is 'accommodation for umbrellas and walking sticks. (Loud laughter.) "- Ernest Rent, M.R.C.V.`6., of Hulme, Manchester, stated that he was. greatly interested in -dogs, more especially in collie and • terrier , breeds, and had great experience as to their value. lie confirmed Mr. Browne's statement as to offering Mrs. Childs two years -ago, £2O for the dog. It was then a very promising puppy seven months old. He had had the skull of the dog sent to him, and oe examining it he was satisfied that it had not deteriorated since the time when it was a,puppy. A good point was that the skull had , not become any thicker since then. He had since the first offer sent a friend to again make ad Offer.to Mrs. Childs,bot she had refused him. , Arthur Shaworosa, M.R.C.V.S., said that the carcase of the deg was sent tobim on April 25th, and he made a post-mortem examination of the body. As far as lie could judge the dog was a,very good one, and had evi- dently -been very healthy. The death was due to irritant poisoning, and there were distinct traces of stychnine. Mr. White, for the defence, said that in the first place he would like to submit that the mere fact , that, the de- feculent kepth licensed beer-house did not justify the public who were invited there impliedly solely fee the purpqse of obtainipg beet, in bringing dogs whei were uninvited. Defendant did not keep an inn, and was not therefore boupd to supply, man and beast. -' The only-Invitation held 'oat to the publin was or them to come in and drink. • • ' The judge remarked that the defendant notwithstand- ing bad a clear, obligation ' upon him, and that was to protect the public against any dangerous thing that he might place in the house. i Mr. White submitted that it was not so in the case Of a beer-house, and further that the plaintiff was bound to prove that the dog was there by the implied invitation of the eefendant. Mr. Kenyon admitted that he bad placed poison in the house, which he had a perfect right to do, and that right was expressly reccgnised by law. His Honour :11 is perfectly true that a man might place poison in his house, but it is at his own esk. Mr. White said that he would be in a position to prose that the dog had not been on the premises for six months prior to the day when it was peisoned. On that occasion it had stolen a leg of mutton, and Mr. Kenyon had expressly requested Mr. Childs on that occasion not to bring the dog there again and Mr.Childs had promised to carry out that request. ?Regarding the vabso of the dog the only. evidence they had was that of Mr. Stent who had offered £2O for the puppy when it was seven months old. He had not Said whether the dog had de- teriorated or not in the body since that time. lie wished to know whether it was likely that workingclass people would keep a dog which was worth £2O, as a common yard dog. They did not even say that it was for breeding purposes. /t had been stated that the dog eiaS a thief, and that was an important matter and affected the value to a great-extent. Having mischiev- ous propensities very materially decreased its value. ' • His Honour: What is ite misehievious propensity? Mr. White: Stealing a leg of mutton I should think. . His Honour: I do not agree with you. You must' Prove that the dog was an habitual thief. Benjamin Kenyon, the defendant, said that he. had held 'the" Green Man "since March 9, 1894. When he entered the houie, he found it infested with mice, and had bought several traps to catch them, but they had failed in their purpose, and he then bought some Battles poison, which contained strychnine. He put some of this on bread and butter', and placed it about the rooms of the house, and some was placed underneath the skirt- ing board of the tap room, wherethe .dog must have got at it. He had never given permission to the plaintiff to bring his dog into the house. He had objected in fact to. dogs coming in,and had turned several away. He had expressly told Mr. Childs not :to bring his dog in again after stealing the leg Of smutton—which' was six months ago, and since then it had not been in until the day when It was poisoned.—By Mr. Briewne : • His house was not the resort of dog fanciers, neither had there been any "sweeps" held there.'He admitted having offered to the defendant £5 to settle the case, but not £lO. Evidence was also given by John Deakio, and John Hatton. • - . Mr. Browne contended that the case was practically undefended, and his Honour, after exhaustively review- ing the evidenne,ded that the law did not prohibit a min placing poison in his houeeb,ut hedid an at his own risk. If it was not properly proteefed h' was responsible for leaving it within reach Of •people or, does which those people might bring into the house:- That-seemed to be the case on this occasion rhad thebnly question to settle was that Qf the damages. /he -value of the dog, hid beim stated at various amounts, and he thought that he would meet the requirements of the case if he gave judg- merit for the plaintiff for £l5. , ,

' A -VALUABLE SUGGESTION. now LADIES CAN. OBTAIN STYLISH GARMENTS WHICH COMBINE GRACE AND BEAUTY _WITH ECONOMY._

If ladies want to sa-ver-money in dresses we advise them to obtain the material at first cost from the Provi- dence Mills Spinning Company and become their own dressmakers. At any rate if a lady cannot make her own costumes she can economise by saving middleman's profits on the material, which amount to threepence or more in each shillingsworth. The above mills supply readers of this paper with dress material in the newest patterns ;fast colours;, strong make; firm cloth ; sty- lish effects and the latest fashion, suitable for any age. In these days of scarcity of cash it is interesting to know the way to buy the necessities of life direct from the manufacturers. We confidently recommend you to write to-day for pstterns, which will be sent free of charge by sending a postcard with your name and ad- dress upon it. These' must be returned at earliest con- venience. Letters should be carefully addressed Pro- vidence Mills Spinning Co., C. Department, Bradford, Yorks. N.13.—1f you like they will send you some beautiful samples of knitting, ernproidery, and fancy wools for home knitting which may be kept by you. Gen'ts sum- mer Bulldogs are also supplied at price! very much below any retail shop.

TWENTY GOALS IN 14 MINUTES. At the Burroughs Gardens Bath, Liverpool, on Thurs. dap,; evening week, Warrington played off their afiOpad engagement in the Liverpool and District Witet, t-rblo Competition, their:opponents being the team represent, jog the Wooltoa SwimmingiClub. Warrington "put in the field " the same team that administered so decisive, a defeat to the crack •North Liver- pool Gymnasium team, the week previous. On the rolhcall . of players being called 'it was found that Woolton bad cOmeqs man short, but this difficulty was surmounted by Warrington lending one of their reserve men, Creighton, who afterwards played , an exceedingly good game'; indeed he was far and away Woelton's best man. ?dr. Finnegan, 9f Liverpool, was referee, whilst Mr. Grace, of ,Warrington, did the duties of time-keeper. . From, the ,• eemreencement to the conclusion of the game, play ;• was nothing more or less than a continual•boarhardment of the Wootton goal, the defenders of ,which were utterly bewildered by , the accurate passing ,and deadly shooting of the Warringtonians. From the Word to-go Locker reached the ball (a lead which he afterwards maintained),, and passed back to Scotson who sent it' well well up the bath to Ashton, who in turn promptly shot through, but the goal was disallowed, Ashton having fouled by standing. A strong shot was well stopped by the Wootton goal4reeper, who, however failed to send , clear and 'Ashton being dandy, sent through, this time wilmut any informality, and after 2t minutes play. • On the restart, Locker's, pass to Scotson and from hina to the forwards saw the ball re- bound from the cross-bar, where it Was secUred by Turner who succeeded in notching number two. The third point fell to the credit of Locker from an audacious pass across the goal mouth. Goals came rapidly from Scotson, Furness, Turner, Locker, and at half-time War- rington were ahead by 8 goals to nil. • •ffhe re-commencement of play saw Warrington with the advantage of the shallow end of the' bath. Locker opened the fusillade by scoring from ,a foul by Wootton, And Scotson desirous of emulating the feats of bis,chlef nearly decapitated the opposing goal-keeper With a, shot which made, the diving board quiver again. Frorn this to the close, the game was a repetition of previotfllay, and at the conclusion:Warrington were credit ad' h the league table with a satisfactory victory of 20 goals to nil: , •• , One of the most effective cures for Scrofula is Scott's Bmul- on. WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT BOWLING LEAGUE. This League, includes the following clubs :—Bull's Head, Cemetery, Horse and Jockey, Liberal Club, and Red Lion isStockton Heath). There is a good working committee,and with a most indefatigable secretary (Mr. ' E. Taylor) and such an enthusiastic lover of this most enjoyable game as Mr. Robert Anderton for president, the league bids fair to become a tower of strength in the town, and to aff rd many pleasant and social meetings on the green sward during the season to its votaries, The committee have already arranged home and home matches between each of the above bowling clubs to be played during the summer, and have also arranged for a handicap main on each green before the matches com- mence. The third of these mains was played on the Liberal Club green, on Saturday, and was highly suc- cessful. Eighty competitors struggled for the supre- macy and with perseverance the committee at the close of the day had the satisfaction of having brought to a conclusion (except two games) an unprecedentedly large main. The foregoing two games were decided on Monday even- ing, when Mr. Robert Fairbrother carried off the first prize (10 prizes were offered), the President acting as reteree on this occasion,and every member acknowledged that their visit to the Liberal Club had been of a mosIN pleasant nature. The next main is at the Bull's Head ' to-day, and anather at the Red Lion next Saturday. The winner of the Ist main at Horse and Jockey was George Brown and the winner of the 2nd main at the Cemetery Hotel was Joseph Gleam The first Of the home and home matches will be played on June 15th.. when the following clubs will be eneaged : —Horse and Jockey v. Bull's Head; Red Lion v. Cemetery, to be played on the green of the first named Club. The fol- lowing gentleman have been elected as patrons of the League :—Messrs. Robert Pierpoint, M.P., Thomas Morris and Councillor John Evans ; vice-president, Mr. W. Mclntyre. Committee :—Messrs. F. Webster, R. Fairbrother, T. Bolton, J. T. Farrington, J. Whitfield, T. Bawers, George Brown,John Bowen' Thomas Pemberton and James Lacker '; hon. treasurer, Mr. J. Whittle, and bon. secretary, Mr. T. Taylor.

WARRINGTON MTSICAE SOCIETY.

OPENING OF PARR HALL.

On Tuesday;lastiweek,the Warrington Musical Society under Mr. Crossley's conductorship, commenced the rehearsal, in the Trinity Schools, of the music intended for performance on the occasion of the opening of the new Concert Hall in September next as announced in our last issue. There was a large attendance of members of both chorus and orchestra and the return of many members who had been absent for some time past was noticeable. Much enthusiasm was shown at this special gathering of the Society's forces, and the energetic manner in which a start was made of the various works decided upeti, not only gives promise that the Society will distinguish itself on the opening day of the ball, but encourages the hope that the society will henceforth both prosper in numbers and add to its laurels as an educa- tional institution. The massive choruses of Handel's " Judas " were attacked with zest and an evident en- joyment of the music by all concerned. The march for chorus and orchestra from Wagner's "Tinnhauser,"and Sullivan's cantata "On Shore and Sea" were also put into practice and the start was in every way encourag- ing. 'lso,„ Crossley announced to the members that arrangerlietits had been made for continuing the prac- tices until towards the end of July and for their resump- tion ih September after the holiday season. A 'meeting of the trustees of the organ belonging to the Musical Society which formerly occupied a place in the old Public Hall was also held during the week and it was resolved to approach the Warrington Town Council with a view to their taking over the instrument and utilising it by incorporating as far as practicable the whole or part in a new and enlarged organ which it is - ' to construct for the Park Hall.

LYIIM BANDS OF TTOPE FESTIVAL

Beautiful weather favoured the 6th annual bands of hope festival, for Lymm and Oughtrington on Thursday. Assembling at the Cross, the procession passed by Eagle Brow, Booth's Hill, New Road, and back to Eagle Brow and the field of Dr. Fox, close by. The procession was formed as follows and presented an appearance gay in the extreme :—Oliver Cromwell on horee,two of his soldiers on ponies ; Lymm Subscription Band, Wesleyan Band of Hope, Baptist Band of Hope, Primitive Band of Hope, Oughtrington and Bollington Bands of Hope, Congrega- tional Band of Hope, Widnes Subscription Prize Band, two jewelled Knights on ponies, Napoleon, old English Soldiers, Robinson Crusoe and Man Friday, Hamlet and Romeo, Jester and Boy-blue, Brigands, Swiss Moun- taineers, Japanese, Chinese, Italians, Scotchmen, Irish- men, Welsh Girls, Red Riding Hood, Flower Girls,Rkipp- ' ing Rope Dancers, Maypole Dancers, Barrister, Morris ' Dancers, Gondoliers, Toreadors, Princes of the Tower, Courtiers, Beefeaters, two Indiau Chiefs on ponies Car- riage containing Queen—Miss F. Brearley, Maids of Honour and Train Bearers. Carriage, Maids of Honour, Carriage, Maids of Honour, Crown and Sceptre Bearers, Buffalo Bill on horse, two Cowboys on ponies, John Bull and Britannia, Seasons, Little Children on Lurries, John Gilpir and Dick Turpin, on ponies. The route was deeply lined with spectators who were not slow in giv- ing vent to their expressions of approval,and some 2000, of them proceeded to the field and arrarged themselves round the barrier which enclosed a large plot of land containing the maypole and the dancing boards. The greater number of them had come by train from Warr- ington and Manchester, but not a few had availed them- selves of the facilities afforded by the Manchester Ship Canal, which thus makes another avenue to the attrac- tions at Lymm. After entering the field the children of the different bands of hope were arrangei in an orderly line round the enclosure and their respective comple- ments were:—Congregational Church Band of H0pe,260 ; Primitive Methodist,6o ; Baptists, 86 ; Oughtringtou and Bollington, 200; Wesleyans, 100 to 120. CORONATION OF THE QUEEN. At the head of the field was the throne, mounted upon a raised dais, and reached by a carpeted pathway. The Queen of the Bands of Hope, Miss F. Brearley, daughter of Mr. J. Brearley, jeweller, was attired in cream crepon trimmed with satin and wore a cloak of maroon velvet trimmed with swansdown. In this dress she looked very charming and quite did justice, by her graceful deportment, to the title of " Her Majesty." Her first duty was that of ascending the throne, which she did at the head of her maids-of-honour, the Misses N. drook- man, A. Barton, A. Winstanley,E.Diamonds, J. Gibbon, E. Moss, J. Wardle, M. Hopkin, N. Marsh, A. Guest, and her body of seven courtiers and beef-eaters. Each little maid-of-honour was dressed in cream muslin, with hats of the same material, and all carried bouquets. When all were placed in position, the maids on the steps of the throne and the courtiers ranged round, a number of children all brightly and prettily decked out with finery, danced the royal maypole dance with commend- able accuracy, the plaiting of the coloured ribbons round the pole being accomplished very successfully. Another troupe, this time of morris daucers,then took the boards and gave great satisfaction by their graceful perform- ance. Following this was the most important of the afternoon's events, viz., the crowning of the queen. Master H. Perrin had the duties of this office accorded him and the manner in which he performed his task of bowing his way with the greatest decorum to the queen's feet, and then placing on her head the jewelled symbol of ;majesty, was a treat to all. He then with equal pre- cision bowed his way out of her preeence and made room for Master H. Leigh, who made the presentation of the sceptre to his gracious sovereign. A skipping rope dance wae then performed, and the programme previous to the interval closed with a " movement" of the queen's subjects. Refreshments partaken of.another maypole dance was shown, following which the proces- sion was re-formed and marched dawn Rush Green, Sandy-lane, to Oughtrington Bridge where the children dispersed for tea. Throughout the afternoon the Lymm Subscription Band conducted by Mr. T. T. Gregsou, and the Widnes Subscription Brass Band led by Mr. Thomas Hynes, alternately discoursed spirited music. In the evening Professor Le Mare gave an excellent ventriloquial entertainment and also a skilful marionette display. Concertina solos were rendered by Mr. J. Worsley and the two bands gave a very large and varied programme of musical selections suitable to the • occa- sion. The carrying out of the arrangements was in the hands of a powerful committee under the chairmanship of the Rev. C. Clay. Messrs. E. Brown and G. Howard acted as hon. secretaries and Mr. J. Dodds as treasurer. These gentlemen and their committee have every reason to feel proud of the undoubted success of the arrange- ments, and the fact that all the circumstances seemed to combine to produce a thoroughly first-class festival was gratifying to onlookers as well and those more deeply interested. This annual occasion at Lymm grows in, popularity and bide fair to soon become as popular as the far-famed Kuutsford May-day.

THE LIBERATOR SOCIETIES.

Meetings were held in London on Thursday of credi- tors aod depositors of the Liberator group of companies, when a scheme was submitted for the realisation of the assets by the transter thereof to a new company. It was proposed to allot a certain number of shares to the credi- tors of the several companies, and to raise £1,310,000 by the issue of 6 per cent, debentures, to be offered first to the depositors and preference shareholders. The scheme it was stated ,applied to £12,000 creditors and depositors of the Liberator Society, about 15,000 creditors and depositors of the House and Land Investment Trust,and about 2,000 preference shareholders in the Lands Allot- ment Company. Mr. Gould, a member of the Liberator committee of inspection, said it would be a disastrous thing if the scheme were not accepted, and the Rev, ,L Stockwell Watts said the scheme was the best pc.ssible. The several classes of creditors passed resolutions agreeing to the proposals. POST OFFECE RED-TAPE.

A correspondent of the " Morning " calla attention to what is, with some show of reason termed a fine point in respect to the action of the postal authorities of a vil- lage in witts, and the protest made by a local nsident against charges Which have been made for the delivery of telegrams, it being contended by him that the house was within the statutory mile of the post-office. The distance having been measured several times it resulted in the following decision :—That the front door and its knocker are within a mile, but the bell and the side door are outside the limit !

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 8, 1895 EARLESTOWN A.N D N EWTON. II SHOCKING, DEATH OF AN EXCURSION' IST AT EARLESTOWN.

ACCIDENT AT THB VIADUCT WORKS.

_ On Wednesday afternoon an old man named Thomas Potts, employed as a labourer at the Viaduct Works was engaged loading timber from a stack about 12 feet high, when he slipped and fell to the ground alighting on his head. He was removed home on the ambulance in an unconscious condition, and attended by Dr. Handysicle who found him , suffering from severe concussion of the brain. ST. MARY AND ST. 60CIETY.

A meeting of the --c-o-riTnii-ttee was held at the depot oa Friday afternoon. There were present the Revs. F. Miller, J. Bartley, and E. R. Davies, Dr. Mouncey. Mr. Dring, and the Secretary (Mr. Peake). The Rev. F. Miller was voted to the chair. The Secretary presented the report of Nurse Jones of her work. She had made a total number of visits of 1,266, and in donations had received £2 6s. Bd. A discussion took place on the work,and the commit- tee considered that Nurse Jones had performed her ' duties very satisfactorily. The number of recom- mend notes issued was 129. The Secretary was in- structed to call the next meeting for the first week in September, and to prepare a list of the subscrip- tions for the committee before making a general appeal to the public. The report was adopted, and a vote of thanks was accorded the Chairman.

Whit-Monday at Earlestown did not pass over with- out affordii g its contribution to the chapter of accidents which usually occur on a popular holiday. About 8.30 that morning when an excursion train from Leeds to Liverpool was travelling at a good speed between New- ton Bridge and Earlestown, a young man was seen to be sitting on a step of one of the carriages of the train. As the train passed over the Wargrave-road bridge, at the end of the curve platform the man was hidden from view by the thick hedge which runs alongside the line, and nothing more was seen of him until about 10 minutes after the train had passed, when the Com- pany's official walking along thh line, saw the body of a man lying there. At first he ' thought the man was drunk, but a closer inspection ' showed that a frightful accident had happened and that the man was dead, his brains lying on the ballast close by the body,and his face badly mutilated. It was after- wards ascertained this was the man seen riding on the footboard of the carriage, and it was supposed he had fallen off as the train was passing over the crossing,and alighting on his head on the pdint rods which at this spot run alongside the line, he had been instantly killed. A L. and N.-W. privilege ticket from Manchester to Liver- pool was found upon him, and later in the day it was ascertained that he was James Ford, an employi of the L. and N.-W. Railway Co., at the goods department, London-road Station,Manchester. On Tuesday the body was identified by the deceased's father and brother, but they could give no information which would clear up the mystery of his being on the carriage step.

THE INQUEST. Mr. S. Brighouse, coroner, held an inquiry into the circumstances attending the death of the man James Ford, who lived at 3 Holden-street, off Oldham-street. Manchester, at the Railway Inn, Earlestown, on Wed- nesday morning. Mr. Hailey, from the solicitors' office, Lime-street, Station, was present on behalf of the Rail- way Company. The first witness was William Ford, father of the de- ceased, who gave evidence of identification, stating that his son was 25 years of age, and was employed in the goods department, at Lmdon-road Station. The Coroner asked Police-Sergeant Banks if there was any report from Liverpool with regard to the else. Mr. Halley said they had.received a report from the guard, which he read. Collinson Richardson Metcalfe, of Leeds, guard of the excursion train, said that as he was going through Park- side he saw a man's hat flying, but he never saw any- thing else. When he arrived at Edge Hill, three people named T. H. Rowse, and A. Duckroyd, of Leeds, and H. Shawcross, of Dewsbury, came to him, and told him that about two miles from Newton-le-Willows a man had jumped out of tlr3 train. At first he thought they were joking, but eventually he took them to the stationmaster at Edge Hill, and took their names and addresses. They told him a man had lost his hat, and that he got out of the carriage and held on by the handle with both hands, and then fell down. He afterwards learned that they had pulled the communication cord, but on the wrong side of the train. The train was travelling at the rate of 30 or 40 miles an hour when they passed Earlestown. Alfred Henderson, of Wargrave-road, Earlestown, de- posed to seeing a man standing on the platform of the train as it passed the bridge. illiam Wildgoose, of Warrington, a telegraph lines- man, deposed to finding the deceased about 8.45. He was dead and very much damaged about the face and unrecognisable. The jury at once returaed a verdict of " Accidental death." After the enquiry, the foreman of the jury (Mr. Att- field) addressing the coroner, said in view of the fact that there had been two accidents recently, the jury had come to the unanimous conclusion that it was abso- lutely. necessary that a mortuary should be provided.— The Coroner agreed with the suggestion and pointed out the desirability of a mortuary for the purpose of holding post-mortem examinations.—The jury then passed a re- commendation to the Newton District Council as to the desirability of erecting a mortuary for the district over which they have control.

GOOD TEMPLAR AT EARLICSTOWN:

The weekly session of the Hope of Earlestown Lodge of Good Templars was held in the Co- operative Hall, on Monday evening. Bro. Wilson presided, and there was a fair muster of members. Several items of business were introduced. and re- I

ferred to the business of the committee. The lodge

was formally closed and afterwards de- clared open, a social evening being spent, games of various kinds being indulged in.—The usual weekly session of the Earlestown Ebenezer Lodge was held in the Co-operative Hall, on Tues- day. Bro. W. J. Taylor, C.T., presided, and there was a good attendance of members and friends. The programme of the evening was then gone through, it being" Sewing Night " for the brothers, Sister Mrs. Brown being responsible. The lodge closed at 9.15, and the members retired to Mr. Smith's chip potato shop where the friends and members partook of a chip potato and tripe supper, which was provided by the brothers and was very much enjoyed. PICNIC FRONI NEWTON. Through the kindness of Mr. A. C. McCorquodale the office staff and foremen in the employ of Me.srs. McCorquodale and Co., at Newton, were enabled to spend a very pleasant outing at Cound Hall,Shrevvs- bury, the residence of- Mr. A. C. McCorquodale. The party left Newton by the 7.30 train and arrived at Shrewsbury about 10.40, where two brakes were waiting to convey them to the Hall. On reaching the Ball they sat down to an excellent lunch, after which they enjoyed themselves in various ways. A cricket match was played in the grounds between the visitors and Cound, tne former being victors. For those who did not play cricket fishing and boat- ing was provided. At four o'clock all sat down to a substantial dinner, served in a marquee in the grounds, and after the meal a convivial hour was spent. The usual loyal toats, and the health of the host was drunk, with much enthusiasm, and the party left for home at-, 7.25, after a most enjoyable out.

On Wednesd-a-Y-ei-enii;g---tVe—USUaI fortnightly meeting of the Samuel Morley Section of Cadets was held in the Ca-operative Hall, Barlestown, at 7 o'clock. Bro. J. Judson presided. One new mem- ber was initiated. The patrons reported that the annual picnic and tea will take place on Saturday, June 29th, at Micklehead Green, near Rainhill. Messrs. Rawlins had kindly placed their school and field at the disposal of the patrons for that date. Bro. James Rigby, of Warrington, was introduced to the Section by Bro. W. Pearson and spoke a few words of encouragement to the Section. The Britons Need met at 8 o'clock in the same room, one new member admitted. Bro. Foden presided. Bro. Judson introduced Bro. Rigby to the Division. Bro. Rigby addressed a few words to the members present. He referred to the early days of teetotal- ism, and showed how Insurance Societies were afraid to admit teetotalers at first which lead to the formation of separate societies. He also referred to the early days of the Order ; showed its growth and stability and the blessing enjoyed by those who joined. Although a member over 20 years he never had been on the funds before. A very pleasant and profitable meeting was closed at 9.30.

ST. PETER'S DAY AND SUNDAY SCHOOLS. The annual treat to the children attending the above schools, and the Gas-st. School took place on Thursday afternoon. Mustering at the school at three o'clock, the children to the number of about GOO, were formed in procession, and headed by the Reformatory band, by the kind permission of Mr. Digweed, who also conducted, marched by way of Golborne-st. and High-st.to St. Peter's Church where a short service was held, and an address given by the Rev. F. W. Johnstone. At the close thev marched back to the school, where tea was served aloput 4.30. After the meal they proceeded to a field lent by Mr. Hayward, where games of various kinds were indulgtd in. Mrs. Royse also organised a number of races for the little ones, for tevs. A couple of balloons were also sent up, to the cheers of the youngsters. The arrangements were effici- ently carried out by Mr.Singletou, Mr. Wykes, Miss Liptrott and the Vicar. LECTURE AT THE WELSH CHAPEL....

NEWTON DISTMCT COUNCIL.

BUSINESS OF THE NEXT MEETING.

A meeting of this Council is convened for Monday next, when the following business will be dealt with : HIGHWAYS, PUBLIC. WORKS, AND SANITARY COMMITTEE. This committee will submit the following minutes— A petition dated March 13th, from a number of rate- payers, asking for the footpath on the east side of Cross- lane to be made in a more permanent manner, was con- sidered, and it was resolved that the same be re-kerbed, flagged, and tiled,in the same manner as other footpaths are now made. Miss E. Lane's letter dated 4th inst., re making of Birley-st.,was read and passed to Mr.Brierley to answer. The report of the sub-committee to consider the mak- ing of private streets, dated 13th May, was read, and after a prolonged discussion on setts and macadam, it ' was adopted. Tenders for horse and cart-hire on the highways were submitted, together with the Surveyor's specification for same, and it was resolved that the tender and prices quoted by Mr. Richard Jones, 6, Bridge-st., be accepted. Tenders for the supply of provender for the horses in the sanitary department, were referred, with samples,to a special committee of Messrs. Ormand, Collingwood and Stone, to decide. The report of Mr. Gregory having been read, it was resolved that the portion ot the report having reference to the tolls from Newton Common during May Queen ' and other festivals, be referred to the Law Clerk to corn- municate with Mr. Pardey. Three cases of scarlatina from one house in Earle-st., having been reported by the Inspector, it was resolved that Dr. Valentine's attention be called to the deloy in reporting the cases, particulars with which we were pre- viously acquainted. Plans for the erection of six houses in Crow-lane and Mercer-st., tor the Royal Good Intent Lodge of Oddfel- lows also tor livery stables and coach houses in Hotel- at., for Mr. McDermott, were submitted and passed. CAS AND WATER COMMITTEE. This Committee will sarnit the fallowing resolutions for confirmation : A letter trom Captain Royse re gas deposits was read, and it was resolved that the gas manager report on same at the nest meeting. • The carbonizing account for the past month was read giving fuel used 235 tens, gas made 2,379,800 feet,being an average of 10,089 feet per ton of fuel used. The gas manager's report and requirements were read, and it was resolved that the same be obtained at com- petitive prices. Quotations for carting to and from the gas works were submitted by Mr. Brierley, and it was resolved that the carting contract be placed with Mr. H. France at the prices now quoted. A letter was read from Mr. George H. Coombs re his water supply and the same was referred to Mr. Brierley to deal with.

FINANCE COMMITTEE. This Committee reports that a summary of the Law Clerk's salary and expenses for the last five years were submitted and ordered to stand over to a future meeting. SCHOOL ATTENDANCE COMMITTEE. This Committee states that the school attendence officer (Mr. Ball) having read his report, it was resolved that as to that portion having reference to Mr. Ball being ordered out of St. Mary's R. C. schools by Father Lennon, a copy be sent him for an explanation.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

(BY AN OCCASIONAL CONTRIBUTOU.)

The school children were very fortunate on Monday in having fine weather for their annual demonstration, and subsequent amusements in the fields. The procession was a formidable one, and the children presented a very pleasing appearance, the numerous banners and hannerettes lending additional gaiety to the scene. , It is a great pity that the Church of England party cannot see their way to take part in the demonstration, but I suppose they have sufficient reasons for their absten'ion.

The sad accident on the railway near Earlestown station on Monday morning, was a very bad opening for the holiday, and appeared to cast a cloud over the day's proceedings. Although the victim did not belong to the neighbouxhood, still the sad affair formed the chief subject of comment during toe day. At the inquest. which was held on Wed- nesday, the jury brought forward a subject, which I have more than once introduced into these columns, viz., the provision of a mortuary for the reception of dead bodies. In this case, a publican refused to make provision, or to receive the body, and the Railway Company were forced to keep it on their premises. A mortuary in a place like Earles- town is a most necessary thing, especially when it is remembered that this is not the first time a dead body has had to be carted about the street from place to place, before shelter could be obtained. if properly erected,the mortuary could be used for the holding ot post inortem examinations, and thus do away with the inconvenience of holding them in private houses. I trust the District Council will give the jury's recommendation the fullest consider- ation, and provide a suitable building without delay.

I see from the report of the nurse attached to the Medical and Surgical Aid Society, that during the time she has held office she has made over a thousand visits to sick people in her capacity of nurse, but has only received from these peoplea little over .£2, as donations. Fancy that! there is thankfulness and plenty of it, but at what an

RUNCORN.

BY OUR RUNCORN CORRESPONDENT.

Runcorn people have for the greater part of this week been holidaying—in fact Whitsuntide is the most popular holiday season of the year with them. Most of the business establishments and works' were closed until Wednesday and some until Thurs- day morning. Thus the workpeople have enjoyed a complete relaxation from toil. Some took ad- vantage of the short date excursions to health re- sorts, and those who stayed in town on Monday were spectators of the annual fete when the town was the scene of music and mirth. The proces- sions were conducted on similar lines to those in previous years and practically the same number of children participated. The Church of England schools had a separate procession from the rest which, by the way, was far from being a sign of Christian unity. If the two processions be looked upon as an exhibition of strength it certainly re- dounded to the credit of the Nonconformists who had by far the largest number of scholars. The weather during the holidays was propitious, and on Tuesday there seemed to be quite a rush to get away from the town to enjoy seaside breezes or hill-climbing at Frodsham.

At a meeting of the Runcorn Council on Thurs- day morning there seemed to be a disposition on the part of the worthy councillors to rush the business off in quick time. The various committees minutes were proposed and seconded with such promptitude and brevity that the members appeared to be in a race against time. One mem- ber in particular did not give himself time to get upon his feet to second a motion but this little informality did not escape the quick eye of the chairman who at once requested him to stand when performing such a duty. This he did with good grace. It was not until the Technical Instruc- tion Committee's minutes were reached that there was a break in this "Jack-in-the-box " way of pro- posing and seconding minutes, but which was far from being unpleasant to some who it may be stated were not councillors.

The interesting announcement was made when the minutes of the Technical Instruction Com- mittee were under discussion that Sir John T. Brunner, Bart., M.P., would open the New Technical Institute on July 17. This will be his first public appearance at Runcorn since his baronetcy was conferred upon him, and he will no doubt receive a most hearty reception. The very sensible letter which he has forwarded to the Rev. J. Simpson in reply to the vote of congratulation passed at the meeting of the Runcorn Noncon- formist ministers, in which he states tbat he hopes the new distinction may be a help and , not a hindrance to his work—an incentive to better things and not a temptation to abandon his prin- ciples—seems to contradict the presumption of a writer in the " New Age of last Thursday who states 1` It may be presumed that Sir John Tomlin- son Brunner will now survey his tall chimneys at Northwich with an air of greater distinction.

For some time past the ferry question has been under the consideration of the authorities at Run- corn and Widnes. The matter was again intro- duced at the meeting of the Runcorn Council yesterday, by the Rev. Dr. Gastaldi. it seems that the Manchester Ship Canal Company are still per- sisting in charging a toll to cross the canal, which with the fee charged for ferrying the river brings the cost of transit from the two counties to 3d. per head. Referring to this a correspondent writes as follows to a Liverpool contemporary: "The addi- tional penny now charged by the Ship Canal Com- pany for crossing the canal in the boat they have provided for the purpose of enabling the terry to continue, which they were compelled to do by their Act of Parliament, makes the fare nothing short of extortionate. The Ship Canal Company have no rights in the ferry conferred by their own Act, or any other Act, and it is argued that the charge is altogether illegal. It is quite certain that the rail- way company will Rot take action in the matter, as their receipts cannot be affected in any way, and the local authorities will have to be the parties to act."—As will be seen from the remarks made by the Law Clerk to the Runcorn Council, action is already being taken, and if the vexed question is not amicably settled before long, an order is to be asked for, at the holding of the next Assizes for the enforcement of the rights of the public respecting the ferry.

MARRIAGE OF MISS BOWDEN, OF F RODRIAM.

SERMON ON CHRISTIAN UNITY AT RUNCORN.

At St. Paul's Wesleyan Church, Runcorn, on Sunday I mining, the Rev. T. Brackenbury referred to the sub- ject of Christian Unity in the course of his sermon. He ' chose for his text" Until the spirit be poured out from on High," Isaiah xxxii. 15. He said not only as indi- viduals but as churches did they need a copious effusion 1, of the Holy Spirit. The Church of Christ in England was composed of several different denominations. They were distinguished by different names, they had differ- I ent forms of the government; they had different modes of worship ; they had each a history of their own : and some of them had distinctive doctrines on which they specially insisted. But if they had faith in Jesus Christ as their redeemer and their Lord, and if they worshipped God in His name, they were all members of Christ's mystical body. Now the great need of all these churches was a larger measure of the Holy Spirit's grace and presence. The presence of the Spirit was needed in order to maintain and manifest their true Christian unity. It was important to understand what they meant by unity. It surely did not mean uniformity in external organisa- tion and government, or conformity to one mode of worhip, or even sameness of theological belief. He took it to mean what the New Testament called "The Unity of the Spirit"—that oneness of affection and sympathy, and hearty co-operation which was created by the common reception of the Holy Spirit of God. They rejoiced to believe that there never was more essential unity among the Nonconformist churches in this country than there was at the present time. They prayed that it might increase and be practically mani- fested by mutual sympathy and helpfulness. But that led them to inquire what was the chief hindrance to Christian unity in this country. It was not the fact that they had one church out of several established and patronised by the State. There might be New Testa- ment unity out of that. It arose from the fact that a large proportion of the clergy in that State church held the Popish doctrine of Apostolic succession. Holding this figment they arrogated to themselves an ecclesiasti- cal superiority. They claimed to be the church in an exclusive and bigoted sense. They unchurched all other Christians and regarded them as schismatics. They , described other denominations as " sects " and used it as a term of opprobrium. They spoke of at- tempts being made to bring the Church down to the level of the " sects." They regarded Nonconformist ministers, however learned, and wise, and devoted, and successful as persons not duly ordained ; as persons who had no right to exercise the functions of the ministry ; as persons who could not properly administer the Christian sacraments. On that assumption many of them adopted an aggressive policy toward Nonconform- ist churches,and would be glad if they could sweep them out of existence in a month. They,the Nonconformists; were quite willing to admit that many whe held this mischievous notion were sincere and good men,and that they really thought they were doing God's service. But as long as they held such a dogma and maintained such an attitude, complete Christian unity was, of course, impossible. What should be their attitude towards ' them? Ehould they as Nonconformists submit to them? No, not for an hour! Should they believe their teach- ing ? No, certainly not ! Should they be silent res- pecting their error ? Again' no. Their charity must not make them indifferent to truth. ' They were so to pray that the Spirit from on high might be poured upon them so that they might no longer cherish the spirit of exclusiveness, but be of one heart and of one soul with all who loved the Lord Jesus Christ.

WHIT-MONDAY.—THE FRIENDLY SOCIETIES' PROCES- sioN.—In accordance with usual custom, there was a procession of members of friendly societies on Whit- Monday. Three societies took part in the procession viz.: The Maiden Queen Lodge of the Modern Druids Society; Court Independence Lodge of the Ancient Order of Foresters • and the Emblem of Hope Lodge of the Grand United Order of Oddfellows. The Foresters led off the procession and they were headed by the Verdin Adelaide Band (Nertbwich). Then followed the Druids, preceded by the Frodsham Church and Vounteer Band; and these were succeeded by the Oddfellows. The lodge members of the Foresters' Society who walked num- bered 40, and amongst those present were :—Bros. Thomas Edge (president),John Holland (vice-president), Thomas Illidge (treasurer), Earlam (auditor) and Alexandra Jun( s (steward). In the second contingent there were amongst others :—Bros. John Garner (secre- tary), Davies (treasurer), E. Aston, J. McWaters (auditors), Dr. Selby (medical officer), John Walker, A. Sutton, and T. Parker. There were about 45 Oddfellows in the procession, including :—Bros. M. G. J. Henshaw, V. G. George, Norcross, T. Gilchrist (secretary), T. Maddock (treasurer), G. A. Nicholls, G. Davenport (auditors). The united precession proceeded to church where a special service was conducted by the Vicar (Rev. H. B. Blogg). Mr. R. Birkett Murgrove presided at the organ. After visiting several residences in the vicinity the men proceeded to their lodge rooms and were dismissed. The sick and funeral fund in con- nection with the Foresters commenced with a balance of £2,361 10s. sd. ; the total income for the year being £2,681 18s. 4d. During the year the members received in sick pay £216 15s. ; funeral allowance for members, , £9O; for members' wives, £24 ; and for members' chil- dren, £3. The management fund commenced with a balance of £7 14s. 6d. and closed with one of £1 14s. sd. The total worth of the lodge is £2,349 17s. 9d. The mem- bership at the end of last year was £216. The Druids Society showed an income of £159 14s. 4d., as well as £4 ss. in donations. The expenditure in sick allowance and deaths was £76 Us. 6d..and for sundries £44 2s. sd. the balance standing at £159 95.. The membership is 140. The receipts for the 'Emblem of Hope lodge were £206 18s. 4d. ; entrance fees £4 17s. ; interest, £3O 108. ; the district funeral fund, £6O; total £302 ss. 4d. The expenditure for sick nay was £177 es. The worth of the society is £1,370. The members number 200. TRINITY WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL. —Th 102nd anniversary in connection with this school was held on Sunday when the Rev. W. H. Parr, of Liverpool, preached morning and evening. He also delivered an address to the scholars, teachers, and friends in the afternoon. Special hymns were sung by the choir and scholars who were under the conductorship of Mr. Sant. The organ was presided at by Mr. Pickering. Good congregations assembled at the services, and the collec- tions amounted to £22 2s. ld. ORGAN RECITAL.—At the Parish Chutch, Frocisham,on Sunday evening,Mr. R. Birkett Musgrove gave an organ recital. The selections performed were from the works of Handel, Haydn, Wagner, and Hill ; and the organist also played a "Triumphal March," which he had com- posed himself. There was a large attendance, and the service was much appreciated.

SHIP CANAL.

A RECORD.

The total number of vessels entering and leaving the Manchester Ship Canal last week was 107, the largest on record. Thirty-eight of the vessels were engaged in the foreign trade, the remaining 69 being coasters. NEW POTATOES FROM JERSEY.

The first steamer to arrive at Manchester with a cargo of this season's crop of potatoes was the s.s. Topaz, chartered by the Goole Steamship Company. This steamer left Jersey at 10.30 p.m. on Friday last with about 250 tong of new petatoes destined for Manchester, Blackburn, Leeds, Liverpool, Warrington, &c. She entered Eastham Locks at 7.15 p.m. on Sunday, and docked at Manchester at midnight. The whole of the cargo was landed and despatched before five o'clock,and every railway waggon containing the consignments was off the dock rremises before 8 a.m. There are now twelve special trains per day which leave the docks direct carrying traffic for the different railway systems serving Lancashire and Yorkshire. The Topaz left the docks at 11 a.m.,and after bunkering at Partington,pro- ceeded to sea, clearing from Eastham at 5 p.m., so that from the time of her entering the canal to the time of her departure she only occupied 22 hours. THE LOCK-OUT OF LABOURERS.

The dock labourers employed on the Salford docks, who persist in wearing the metal badge as a token of their membership of the National Union of Dock Labourers, are still refused employment by the Canal Company, and though a few have given way to the desire of their employers the majority refuse. The dock gates at Salford are guarded by extra constables. A large number of men have been brought from other places on the canal, and though there is at present a large number of vessels receiving and discharging cargo the work is being carried on with but very slight incon- veniene. The dispute has not so far affected the la- bourers employed on the Pomona docks, where unionists and others are employed side by side.

RUNCORN LABOURERS AND THE SALFORD DOCKERS' DISPUTE.

On Monday and Tuesday a number of unemployed labeurers at Runcorn were dispatched in steam tugboats to the Salford docks where they have been found employ- ment owing to a dispute which exists through the dock labourers persisting in the wearing of a metal badge as a token of their membership of the N.tional Union of Dock Labourers. The men from Runcorn who were en- gaged by Captain Beesley, do not belong to any Union. Fhey will be brought back again to Runcorn along the Canal on Saturday, and in all probability re-engaged on Monday. Placards have been posted in the town by the Docckers Umi,..n asking labourers to keep away from the Salford docks during the dispute.

A shoemaker, named William Agnew, is in custody of the Glasgow police charged with the murder of his wife, whose body, lying in a pool of blood, with five wounds on the head, was found in their house at 17, Miller- place, on Monday morning. A baker, named Walker, who lodged in Agnew's house, has also been arrested on uspicion. The entire sum considered necessary to defray the cost of the balloon expedition to the North Pole proposed to be undertaken by M. Andree, a Swedish engineer, has now been secured. .

The Liverpool City Council on Wednesday agreed to expend £3,000 on the completion of the panels on the outside of S. George's Hall.

By title these volumes are the " Renascence-cif the English Drama," by Mr. Henry ArthliiJonesourd"Studies of Men,"by Mr. George W. Smalley. The first of thew two is compiled by al gentleman- whcr is r too well known for, th e necessity of an introduction to the public, and mainly as the author of such..pcmular, playa as "The Temptext7,o " Judah," 4' The- Middleman," "The due , ous Susan.? &c., &a., This Renascence dmpossd- pf essays, .lectures.-and fragments relating to the ,moderri English stage, inclall written or delivered ,in the years from andlietween 1883-94. In his pre- face the author says that in producing this volury he has -fought for a redognitioa of the distinction between the art of the drama on the one hand and ` popular amusement qn the ,ether,and of the greater pleasure to be derived from the art of the drama. Hellas fought for the entire freedom of the-modern dramatist; for his right td portray all aspectli of human life, all passions, all opinions; and' also a freedom .of search, phrase, and treatment such as is allowed the Bible and to Shakespeare, The universality of wholesomeness, largeness, and breadth of view it has also been his effort to attain. The source of each essay, its date, and that also of the addresses is given, the majority being from the "Nineteenth Century Review," and the New. Review," and all are specimens of the skilful treat, ment expected from such prominent journals. As a record of all'varieties of opinion on the topic of English Drama, the book is invaluable, and will help forward the project of the author in the minds of all its readers.: The " get-up "of the book is of -first class order, and does credit to its producers. " Studies of Men," a most handsome book, con- tains infnrmation of prominent men which to all people, political or otherwise, is as necessary to their educatiOn allnost as the three It's. Most of the studies have appeared in the "Naw,York Tribune," while the longest, that of a visit to Prinee Bismarck, is perrnissedly re-printed from the" Fortnightly Re- v iew."There are altogether tweitty-aix of the studies, and among the" world's helmsmen" reviewed there are the greatest politicians, ministers and clergy, authors and artistes,in Whose lives,traits, and pos- sibilities, everyone takes an interest. "Studies of men:" is not a volume about which much can be said descriptively,(- as its contents are valuable, chiefly from being the analysis of so many eminent personages, ,alid not :):from any literary originality which it may contain.—The price is nominally high, but when compared to the article for sale sinks at once into triviality. and there is therefore nothing to deplore on that score.. 011ORGE NEWPIBB AND Co.—The present craze for magazines of views, and photographs of famous and tidautiful places has led to some bold ventures in order to attain the firet place, which we think will , -new be accorded the 'above publishers for their -superb coloured reproduction of famous views of the world. WEEKLY CHAT.—Subject : Second-hand Furniture.— The opening of Spring is the time for the busy house- wife to put her house in order, therefore the matter of furni- shing must be well discussed. We cannot, therefore, do better than advise a visit to Worsley's before definitely de- riding where and what to purchase. This visit cannot pos- sibly but prove remunerative, if only in experience. " The Pantheon " being a hive of industry, it is a palatial pile of buildings close tot he Central Station. having a frontage of nearly 150 feet This immense building is filled from floor to ceiling (six storeys) with the grandest collection of High- class Second-hand Furniture in the World,. MI6 is not an exaggeration, but can be verified by ocular demonstration if you pay a visit to Manchester. Suites of Bedroom Furni- ture, for instance, by such makers as Goodall. Lamb, Ogden, ac., original cost from £2OO to £3OO, for from £5O to £7O ; suites, original cost £5O to £7O, for £lO to £2O. Pay a visit and see for yourself ; you can surely use your common sense, and see if Worsley's is the place as described.

FllO COUNTRY BUYERS.—The enormous patronage en- joyed by Worsley's from customers all over Lane ishire, Yorkshire, Cheshire, The Potteries. all over the North, The Midlaads, and Wales, will prove how much is the saving effected by purchasing at Wcusley's. In future carriage will be paid on all orders over £2O to any part of England and Wales. It I. only fair to say that better value could not be obtained than Wors!ey's New Goods and the Second-hand Furniture simply bewilders and demoralises competition. Never mind what distance 300 live from Manchester, it will pay you to visit Worsley's ; you will save quite 50 per cent.

IAUTION.—Beware of buying new London jerrybuilt fur- kJ no matter how low the price is- it is dear at any price. Worsley's have second-hand reliable furniture by good makers, which will last for ages. There is no disputing the fact that high-class second-hand furniture is better than new. The only fault Is that sometimes the article Is old- fashioned. This is easily remedied at Woraley's, they having over 50 cabinet makers at work modernising second-hand fur- niture when necessary. For instance, second-handl side- boards are easily modernised by having the backs raised, the plates bevelled, and the panels carved. You never need fear cracking, blistering, grub eaten, or moth eaten furniture at Worsley's. Second-hand furniture is not troubled with these complaints. They give a written warranty for 10 years with all furnishhing orders.

IJEW FURNITURE.—AII Worley's new furniture is their own make. Of course it is obviously impossible to sup- ply every requirement second-hand, Woreley's have, there- fore, erected a Cabinet Works in City-road, with an immense stock of seasoned timber for the production of reliable furni- ture. Every item in the Bedding Department is new, guaran- teed pure, under minute inspection.
